Definition 4.14
.

Let
x\in X
be such that
x
and
x^{*}
are in different
\mathbb{R}
-orbits. Define a subspace

L_{x}\coloneqq\{\mathfrak{f}\in T_{X}:f(t)=x\;\forall\;t\in[0,\ell(f)]\}.

The
x
-axis
of
T_{X}
is the subspace
\mathbf{L}_{x}\coloneqq L_{x}\cup L_{x^{*}}
. A subspace
L\subseteq T_{X}
is a
standard axis
if it is an
x
-axis for some
x\in X
such that
\Stab_{\mathbb{R}}(x)
is non-trivial and
x^{*}\notin\mathbb{R}\cdot x
.

The lemma below follows immediately from the relevant definitions.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
x\in X
be such that
x
and
x^{*}
are in different
\mathbb{R}
-orbits. Then

\Stab_{T_{X}}(\mathbf{L}_{x})=\{\mathfrak{f}\in\mathbf{L}_{x}:\ell(\mathfrak{f})\in\Stab_{\mathbb{R}}(x)\}\cong\Stab_{\mathbb{R}}(x).

Moreover, if
\varphi:\mathbf{L}_{x}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the map defined by
\varphi(\mathfrak{f})=\ell(\mathfrak{f})
if
\mathfrak{f}\in L_{x}
and
\varphi(\mathfrak{f})=-\ell(\mathfrak{f})
otherwise, then
\varphi
is a
\Stab_{T_{X}}(\mathbf{L}_{X})
-equivariant isometry.

Corollary 4.16
.

Standard axes in
T_{X}
are axes in the sense of Definition
4.11
.

Remark 4.17
.

Clearly ‘most’ lines in
T_{X}
will have trivial stabilisers. Moreover it is not difficult to construct non-standard axes with cyclic stabilisers and even such that the generator of the stabiliser acts on its axis with arbitrary translation length.

A more surprising observation is that it is also possible for a non-standard axis of
T_{X}
to have a dense stabiliser. Suppose that, for some uncountable proper subgroup
H\lneq\mathbb{R}
and
x,y\in X
, the orbits
\mathbb{R}\cdot x,\mathbb{R}\cdot x^{*},\mathbb{R}\cdot y,\mathbb{R}\cdot y^{*}
are pairwise disjoint and
\Stab_{\mathbb{R}}(x)=\Stab_{\mathbb{R}}(y)=H
. Let
L\subseteq T_{X}
be the set of elements of the form
f:[0,\ell]\rightarrow X
such that
f(t)=x
if
t\in H
and
f(t)=y
otherwise and let
\mathbf{L}\coloneqq L\cup L^{*}
. The fact that
H
is uncountable implies that
\mathbf{L}
is not standard, yet
\Stab_{T_{X}}(\mathbf{L})=\{\mathfrak{f}\in L:\ell(\mathfrak{f})\in H\}\cong H
. This behaviour will disappear when we pass to more sensible subgroups of
T_{X}
.

4.3
Templates

We can now introduce the subgroup of
T_{X}
which will be used to prove Theorem
4.1
.

Let
S\subseteq T_{X}
be a set. The closed subgroup
\overline{\langle S\rangle}
generated by
S
is the smallest closed subgroup of
T_{X}
containing
S
, with respect to the topology induced by the metric
d
.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
S\subseteq T_{X}
be a symmetric set which is closed under restriction (i.e.
\mathfrak{s}^{-1}\in S
for all
\mathfrak{s}\in S
and, if the equivalence class of
s:[0,\ell]\rightarrow X
is in
S
, then the equivalence class of the restriction
s|_{[0,t]}
is in
S
for all
t\in[0,\ell]
). Then
\langle S\rangle
and
\overline{\langle S\rangle}
are connected.

Proof.

Let
H\leq T_{X}
be a subgroup and
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}\in H
be elements which are connected to
\id
via paths
\gamma_{\mathfrak{f}},\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}:[0,1]\rightarrow H
. Then the composition
\mathfrak{f}\star\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}
is a path from
\mathfrak{f}
to
\mathfrak{f}\star\mathfrak{g}
and the concatenation of
\gamma_{\mathfrak{f}}
with
\mathfrak{f}\star\gamma_{\mathfrak{g}}
is a path from
\id
to
\mathfrak{f}\star\mathfrak{g}
. It follows that
\langle S\rangle
is connected. Now suppose that
(\mathfrak{f}_{n})_{n\in N}\subseteq H
is a sequence which converges to
\mathfrak{f}\in H
such that each
\mathfrak{f}_{n}
is connected to
\id
via a path in
H
. Then we can assume without loss of generality that
\mathfrak{f}_{n}\preceq\mathfrak{f}_{n+1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and fix geodesics
\gamma_{n}:[0,\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{n})]\rightarrow H
connecting
\id
to
\mathfrak{f}_{n}
. Let
\gamma(\ell(\mathfrak{f}))=\mathfrak{f}
and, if
0\leq t<\ell(f)
, let
\gamma(t)=\gamma_{n}(t)
for some (equivalently any)
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{n})>t
. Then
\gamma:[0,\ell(\mathfrak{f})]\rightarrow H
is a path connecting
\id
to
\mathfrak{f}
. It follows that
\overline{\langle S\rangle}
is connected.
∎

Definition 4.19
.

Given a subset
Y\subseteq X
, let
T_{X}(Y)\coloneqq\overline{\langle S\rangle}
, where
S=\cup_{y\in Y}L_{y}
.

We will show that
T_{X}(Y)
is the universal real tree with valence
2^{\aleph_{0}}
whenever
2\leq|\mathbb{R}\cdot Y|\leq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
. To do this, we will first characterise the elements of
T_{X}(Y)
using
templates
. We start by showing that
T_{X}(Y)=T_{X}(\mathbb{R}\cdot Y)=T_{X}(Y^{*})=T_{X}(\mathbb{R}\cdot Y^{*})
.

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
Y\subseteq X
be non-empty and suppose that
\Stab_{\mathbb{R}}(y)\neq\{0\}
and
y^{*}\notin\mathbb{R}\cdot y
for all
y\in Y
. If
H\leq T_{X}
is a closed subgroup containing
L_{y}
for each
y\in Y
, then
H
contains
L_{\lambda\cdot y}
and
L_{\lambda\cdot y^{*}}
for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Fix
y\in Y
and, for each
\ell>0
, let
\mathfrak{f}_{\ell}\in L_{y}
denote the constant element with length
\ell
and image
y
. Suppose
s\in\Stab_{\mathbb{R}}(y)
and
s>0
. Then
\mathfrak{f}_{s}^{-1}\in H
has length
s
and
f_{s}^{-1}(t)=(-s)\cdot y^{*}=(s\cdot y)^{*}=y^{*}
for any
t\in[0,s]
. Given any
\ell>0
let
s,t\geq 0
be such that
s\in\Stab_{\mathbb{R}}(y)
and
\ell=s-t
. Then
\mathfrak{f}_{s}^{-1}\star\mathfrak{f}_{t}=\mathfrak{f}_{\ell}^{*}\in L_{y^{*}}
so
L_{y*}\subseteq H
.
Now let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and suppose that
\lambda\geq 0
. For each
\ell\geq 0
, let
\mathfrak{g}_{\ell}\coloneqq\mathfrak{f}_{\lambda}^{-1}\star\mathfrak{f}_{\lambda+\ell}
.
Since
\mathfrak{f}_{\lambda+\ell}=\mathfrak{f}_{\lambda}\square\lambda\cdot\mathfrak{f}_{\ell}
, the element
\mathfrak{g}_{\ell}\in H
has length
\ell
and
g_{\ell}(t)=\lambda\cdot y
for all
t\in[0,\lambda]
. Thus
L_{\lambda\cdot y}\subseteq H
. Suppose that
\lambda<0
. For any
\ell>0
, if
g_{\ell}^{\prime}\coloneqq(\mathfrak{f}^{*}_{-\lambda})^{-1}\star\mathfrak{f}^{*}_{\ell-\lambda}
then
g_{\ell}^{\prime}
has length
\ell
and
g_{\ell}^{\prime}(t)=(-\lambda)\cdot y^{*}=(\lambda\cdot y)^{*}
. Thus
L_{(\lambda\cdot y)^{*}}\subseteq H
and by the argument above this implies that
L_{\lambda\cdot y}\subseteq H
.
∎

Definition 4.21

(Templates)
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
with
\ell\geq 0
. A countable set
P\subseteq[0,\ell]
is a
template
of
[0,\ell]
if it satisfies the following.

(T1)

If
p\in P
and
p>0
then there exist
p_{1}\in P
– called the
predecessor
of
p
– such that
p_{1}<p
and
P\cap(p_{1},p)=\emptyset
. If
p\in P
and
p<\ell
then there exists
p_{2}\in P
– called the
successor
of
p
– such that
p<p_{2}
and
P\cap(p,p_{2})=\emptyset
.

(T2)

The union
\cup\{[p,p^{\prime}]:p,p^{\prime}\in P
and
P\cap(p,p^{\prime})=\emptyset\}
has countable complement in
[0,\ell]
.

Given a template
P\subseteq[0,\ell]
, the
inverse
of
P
is the template
P^{-1}\coloneqq\{\ell-p:p\in P\}
.

Note that (T2) implies that every point in the complement is an accumulation point of
P
and that the closure
\overline{P}
is countable.

Let
\ell\geq 0
and
P\subseteq[0,\ell]
be a template. There are two related but slightly different ways of “filling in” a template to produce an element of
\mathcal{Y}_{X}
or
T_{X}
. In the first version, a sequence of elements of
X
, indexed by
P
, directly defines a map. In the second, the input is a suitable sequence of elements of
\mathcal{Y}_{X}
, also indexed by
P
, and the map they define should be viewed as an infinite concatenation (so the action of
\mathbb{R}
must be taken into account).

Definition 4.22

(Realising sequences)
.

(i)

Let
(x_{p})_{p\in P}
be a sequence in
X
. The
realisation
of
(x_{p})_{p\in P}
is the equivalence class of any function
f:[0,\ell]\rightarrow X
such that
f(t)=x_{p}
if
t\in(p,p^{\prime})
for some
p,p^{\prime}\in P
such that
P\cap(p,p^{\prime})=\emptyset
. We say that
(x_{p})_{p\in P}
is
admissible
if
\mathfrak{f}\in T_{X}
.

(ii)

A sequence
(\mathfrak{f}_{p})_{p\in P}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{X}
is
consistent
if the following holds. For each
p\in P-\{\ell\}
, if
p^{\prime}\in P
is the successor of
p
, then
\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{p})=p^{\prime}-p
and if
\ell\in P
then
\mathfrak{f}_{\ell}=\id
. The
concatenation
of
(\mathfrak{f}_{p})_{p\in P}
is the equivalence class of any function
f:[0,\ell]\rightarrow X
such that
f(t)=-p\cdot f_{p}(t-p)
if
t\in(p,p^{\prime})
for some
p,p^{\prime}\in P
such that
P\cap(p,p^{\prime})=\emptyset
. We say that
(\mathfrak{f}_{p})_{p\in P}
is
admissible
if
\mathfrak{f}\in T_{X}
.

Definition 4.23

(Template ore)
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
be the set of realisations of sequences
(x_{p})_{p\in P}\subseteq X
, where
P\subseteq[0,\ell]
is a template and
\ell\geq 0
. The set
\mathcal{Z}_{X}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{X}
is called the
template ore
over X.

One checks easily that
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
is closed under the operations
\square
and
-1
, and under the relation
\preceq
. Moreover the element
\id
is the realisation of any sequence
(x_{0})
so
\id\in\mathcal{Z}
. Therefore the following lemma follows immediately from Lemma
4.7
.

Lemma 4.24
.

(\mathcal{Z}_{X},\preceq,\id,\square,-1)
is an ore.

Definition 4.25

(Complexity)
.

Given
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{Z}_{X}
, the
complexity
of
\mathfrak{f}
is the smallest countable ordinal
\alpha
such that
\mathfrak{f}
is the realisation of a sequence in
X
indexed over a template
P
whose closure has CB-rank
\alpha
. For each countable ordinal
\alpha
, we denote by
\mathcal{Z}_{X}^{[\alpha]}
the set of elements of
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
with complexity
\leq\alpha
.

Remark 4.26
.

The closure of a template is a countable compact Polish space, so its CB-rank is a successor ordinal (see Remark
2.17
). Therefore the complexity of an element of
\mathcal{Z}_{X}
is always a successor ordinal. We will use this liberally from now on.

Lemma 4.27

(Inversion)
.

Let
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{Z}_{X}
be the realisation (resp. concatenation) of a sequence in
X
(resp. in
\mathcal{Y}_{X}
) indexed over a template
P\subseteq[0,\ell(\mathfrak{f})]
. Then
\mathfrak{f}^{-1}
is the realisation (resp. concatenation) of a sequence in
X
(resp. in
\mathcal{Y}_{X}
) indexed over
P^{-1}
.

Proof.

Let
(x_{p})_{p\in P}\subseteq X
be a sequence with realisation
\mathfrak{f}
.
For each
p^{\prime}=\ell-q\in P^{-1}-\{\ell\}
, let
p\in P
be the predecessor of
q\in P
, and let
z_{p^{\prime}}\coloneqq-\ell\cdot x_{p}^{*}
. If
\ell\in P^{-1}
then let
z_{\ell}\in X
be an arbitrary element.
Then
\mathfrak{f}^{-1}
is the realisation of
(z_{p^{\prime}})_{p^{\prime}\in P^{-1}}
.

Let
(\mathfrak{f}_{p})_{p\in P}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{X}
be a consistent sequence with realisation
\mathfrak{f}
.
For each
p^{\prime}=\ell-q\in P^{-1}-\{\ell\}
, where
q\in P
is the successor of
p\in P
, let
\mathfrak{g}_{p^{\prime}}\coloneq\mathfrak{f}_{p}^{-1}
. If
\ell\in P^{-1}
then let
\mathfrak{g}_{\ell}\coloneqq\id
.
Then
\mathfrak{f}^{-1}
is the realisation of
(\mathfrak{g}_{p^{\prime}})_{p^{\prime}\in P^{-1}}
.
∎

Lemma 4.28

(Refining)
.

Let
P\subseteq[0,\ell]
be a template, let
(x_{p})_{p\in P}\subseteq X
be a sequence and let
(\mathfrak{f}_{p})_{p\in P}\subseteq T_{X}
be consistent. For any
t\in[0,\ell]
, there exists a template
Q\subseteq[0,\ell]
, a sequence
(z_{q})_{q\in Q}\subseteq X
and a consistent sequence
(\mathfrak{g}_{q})_{q\in Q}\subseteq T_{X}
such that:

•

t
is an accumulation point of
Q
(in particular,
t\notin Q
);

•

\overline{Q}-Q=(\overline{P}-P)\cup\{t\}
;

•

the realisation of
(z_{q})_{q\in Q}
is equivalent to the realisation of
(x_{p})_{p\in P}
;

•

the concatenation of
(\mathfrak{g}_{q})_{q\in Q}
is equivalent to the concatenation of
(\mathfrak{f}_{p})_{p\in P}
.

The process of replacing
P
with
Q
and either replacing
(x_{p})_{p\in P}
with
(z_{q})_{q\in Q}
or
(\mathfrak{f}_{p})_{p\in P}
with
(\mathfrak{g}_{q})_{q\in Q}
is called
refining
. The fact that
\overline{P}-P\subseteq\overline{Q}-Q
ensures that, by repeating this process a finite number of times,
P
can be refined to admit any finite set of points in
[0,\ell]
as accumulation points.

Proof.

Suppose that
t
is not an accumulation point of
P
and
t\notin\{0,\ell\}
. Then there exists
p_{1},p_{2}\in P
such that
p_{1}<t<p_{2}
and
P\cap(p_{1},p_{2})\subseteq\{t\}
. Let
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(p_{1},t)
be a strictly increasing sequence which converges towards
t
, let
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(t,p_{2})
be a strictly decreasing sequence which converge towards
t
and let
Q\coloneqq(P-\{t\})\cup\{t_{n},s_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Then
Q
is a template with non-trivial accumulation point
t
and
\partial Q=\partial P\cup\{t\}
.

For each
p\in P-\{t\}
let
z_{p}\coloneqq x_{p}
and for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
z_{t_{n}}\coloneqq x_{p_{1}}
and
z_{s_{n}}\coloneqq x_{t}
if
t\in P
and
z_{s_{n}}\coloneqq x_{p_{1}}
otherwise. Then the realisation of
(z_{q})_{q\in Q}
is equivalent to the realisation of
(x_{p})_{p\in P}
. For each
p\in P-\{p_{1},t\}
let
g_{p}\coloneqq f_{p}
. Let
g_{p_{1}}\coloneqq f_{p_{1}}|_{[0,t_{1}-p_{1}]}
and let
g_{s_{1}}:[0,p_{2}-s_{1}]\rightarrow X
be defined by
g_{s_{1}}(s)=(s_{1}-t)\cdot f_{t}(s+s_{1}-t)
if
t\in P
and
g_{s_{1}}(s)=(s_{1}-p_{1})\cdot f_{p_{1}}(t+s_{1}-p_{1})
otherwise. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
let
g_{t_{n}}:[0,t_{n+1}-t_{n}]\rightarrow X
be defined by
g_{t_{n}}(s)=(t_{n}-p_{1})\cdot f_{p_{1}}(s+t_{n}-p_{1})
and, if
n\geq 2
, let
g_{s_{n}}:[0,s_{n-1}-s_{n}]\rightarrow X
be defined by
g_{s_{n}}(s)=(s_{n}-t)\cdot f_{t}(s+s_{n}-t)
if
t\in P
and
g_{s_{n}}(s)=(s_{n}-p_{1})\cdot f_{p_{1}}(s+s_{n}-p_{1})
otherwise. Then
(\mathfrak{g}_{q})_{q\in Q}
is consistent and its concatenation is equivalent to the concatenation of
(\mathfrak{f}_{p})_{p\in P}
.

If
t=0
or
\ell
then perform the above operation but only on one side of
t
.
∎

Proposition 4.29
.

Let
Y\subseteq X
be such that
\Stab_{\mathbb{R}}(y)\neq\{0\}
and
y^{*}\notin\mathbb{R}\cdot y
for all
y\in Y
. Then
T_{X}(Y)
is the set of realisations of admissible sequences
(y_{p})_{p\in P}
in
\mathbb{R}\cdot(Y\cup Y^{*})
, where
P
is a template.

Proof.

We start by showing that the set of all such realisations is a closed subgroup. Lemma
4.27
implies that it is closed under taking inverses. Let
\ell,\ell^{\prime}>0
, let
P\subseteq[0,\ell]
,
P^{\prime}\subseteq[0,\ell^{\prime}]
be templates and let
(y_{p})_{p\in P},(y_{p^{\prime}}^{\prime})_{p^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}\cdot(Y\cup Y^{*})
be admissible sequences with realisations
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
respectively. Let
\tau\coloneqq\ell(\mathfrak{f}\dotdiv(\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\wedge\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})^{-1})
.
By refining
P,P^{\prime}
and
(y_{p})_{p\in P},(y_{p^{\prime}}^{\prime})_{p^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}}
if necessary, we can assume that
\tau
is a non-trivial accumulation point of
P
and
\ell-\tau
is a non-trivial accumulation point of
P^{\prime}
.
Let
P^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq(P\cap[0,\tau])\cup([\ell-\tau,\ell(\mathfrak{f}\star\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})]\cap\{p^{\prime}-\ell+2\tau:p^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}\})
. Then
P^{\prime\prime}
is a template of
[0,\ell(\mathfrak{f}\star\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})]
. For each
p\in P\cap P^{\prime\prime}
, let
x_{p}\coloneqq y_{p}
and for each
p^{\prime}-\ell+2\tau\in P^{\prime\prime}-P
let
x_{p^{\prime}-\ell+2\tau}\coloneqq(\ell-2\tau)\cdot y_{p^{\prime}}^{\prime}
. Then
(x_{p^{\prime\prime}})_{p^{\prime\prime}\in P^{\prime\prime}}
is admissible and the realisation of this sequence is
\mathfrak{f}\star\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
.

Observation 1
.

If
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}\in T_{X}
are such that
\mathfrak{g}\preceq\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{f}
is the realisation of a sequence
(x_{p})_{p\in P}\subseteq\mathbb{R}\cdot(Y\cup Y^{*})
, where
P\subseteq[0,\ell(\mathfrak{f})]
is a template, then
\mathfrak{g}
is the realisation of a sequence in
\mathbb{R}\cdot(Y\cup Y^{*})
indexed over a template. Indeed, up to refining
P
and
(x_{p})_{p\in P}
, we can assume that
\ell(\mathfrak{g})\in\overline{P}
. Let
P^{\prime}\coloneqq\{p\in P:p\leq\ell(\mathfrak{g})\}
. Then
\mathfrak{g}
is the realisation of
(x_{p})_{p\in P^{\prime}}
.

Now let
(\mathfrak{f}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq T_{X}
be a sequence converging to a point
\mathfrak{f}\in T_{X}
such that, for each
n
, if
\ell_{n}\coloneqq\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{n})
then there is a template
P_{n}\subseteq[0,\ell_{n}]
and an admissible sequence
y^{(n)}=(y_{p}^{(n)})_{p\in P_{n}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}\cdot(Y\cup Y^{*})
such that
\mathfrak{f}_{n}
is the realisation of
y^{(n)}
. If
\ell\coloneqq\ell(\mathfrak{f})
then
(\ell_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
\ell
. Take a subsequence of
(\mathfrak{f}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
so that
(\ell_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is either strictly increasing or strictly decreasing. If
(\ell_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is strictly decreasing then
\mathfrak{f}\preceq\mathfrak{f}_{n}
for almost all
n
, so
\mathfrak{f}
is the realisation of some sequence in
\mathbb{R}\cdot(Y\cup Y^{*})
by Observation
1
.
So suppose that
(\ell_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is strictly increasing. By Observation
1
again, we can replace each
\mathfrak{f}_{n}
with a
\preceq
-smaller element so that
\mathfrak{f}_{n}\preceq\mathfrak{f}_{n+1}
for each
n
. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, refine
P_{n}
and
y^{(n)}
finitely many times so that
\ell_{m}
is a limit point of
P_{n}
for all
m\leq n
. Define

P\coloneqq\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}P_{n}\cap(\ell_{n-1},\ell_{n}).

Then
P
is a template of
[0,\ell]
.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and each
p\in P\cap(\ell_{n-1},\ell_{n})
let
z_{p}\coloneqq y_{p}^{(n)}
. Then
(z_{p})_{p\in P}
is an admissible sequence whose realisation is
\mathfrak{f}
.

By Lemma
4.20
, the fact that all realisations of admissible sequences in
\mathbb{R}\cdot(Y\cup Y^{*})
are in
T_{X}(Y)
follows from Lemma
4.30
below.
∎

Lemma 4.30
.

Let
H\leq T_{X}
be a closed subgroup. Let
\ell>0
and let
P\subseteq[0,\ell]
be a template. Suppose that
(\mathfrak{h}_{p})_{p\in P}\subseteq H
is a consistent and admissible sequence and let
\mathfrak{f}
be the concatenation of
(\mathfrak{h}_{p})_{p\in P}
. Then
\mathfrak{f}\in H
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma by transfinite induction on the Cantor–Bendixson rank
\alpha
of
\overline{P}
.

If
\alpha=1
, then
\overline{P}
is a compact set of isolated points and is therefore finite. Therefore
\mathfrak{f}
is a finite concatenation of elements in
H
and is itself in
H
.

Suppose that
\alpha>1
and recall that
\alpha=\beta+1
for some countable ordinal
\beta\geq 1
.
Let
K\coloneqq\overline{P}^{(\beta)}
. Then
K
is a compact set of isolated points so
K
is finite.
Let
k_{0},\dots,k_{m}
be the elements of
K\cup\{0,\ell\}
, ordered such that
0=k_{0}<k_{1}<\dots<k_{m}=\ell
. For each
0\leq i<m
choose a point
k_{i}^{+}=k_{i+1}^{-}\in(k_{i},k_{i+1})\cap P
(this exists by (T2) because
K\cap P=\emptyset
). Let
f_{k_{i}}^{+}:[0,k_{i}^{+}-k_{i}]\rightarrow X
be defined by
f_{k_{i}}^{+}(t)=k_{i}\cdot f(t+k_{i})
and let
f_{k_{i+1}}^{-}:[0,k_{i+1}-k_{i+1}^{-}]\rightarrow X
be defined by
f_{k_{i+1}}^{-}(t)=k_{i+1}^{-}\cdot f(t+k_{i+1}^{-})
.
If
\mathfrak{f}_{k_{i}}^{-},\mathfrak{f}_{k_{i+1}}^{+}\in H
for each
0\leq i<m
then
\mathfrak{f}=\mathfrak{f}_{k_{0}}^{+}\star\mathfrak{f}_{k_{1}}^{-}\star\mathfrak{f}_{k_{1}}^{+}\star\dots\star\mathfrak{f}_{k_{m-1}}^{+}\star\mathfrak{f}_{k_{m}}^{-}\in H
. It therefore remains to show that
\mathfrak{f}_{k_{i}}^{-},\mathfrak{f}_{k_{i+1}}^{+}
are indeed elements of
H
for each
i
.

Fix
k\in\{k_{i}:0<i\leq m\}
and let
Q\coloneqq\{p-k^{-}:p\in P\cap[k^{-},k)\}
. Then
Q
is a template of
[0,k-k^{-}]
and
0\in Q
. If
k=\ell
and
\ell\notin K
then the CB-rank of
\overline{Q}
is at most
\beta
and
\mathfrak{f}_{k}^{-}
is the concatenation of
(\mathfrak{h}_{p})_{p-k^{-}\in Q}
so
\mathfrak{f}_{k}^{-}\in H
by the induction hypothesis. So assume that
k\in K
. Then
\overline{Q}^{(\beta)}=\{k-k^{-}\}
and there exists a strictly increasing sequence
(q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq Q
which converges towards
k-k^{-}
(because
\beta\geq 1
). For each
n
, let
Q_{n}\coloneqq Q\cap[0,q_{n}]
, let
\beta_{n}
be the CB-rank of
\overline{Q}_{n}
and let
g_{n}\coloneqq f_{k}^{-}|_{[0,q_{n}]}
. Then
Q_{n}
is a template of
[0,q_{n}]
,
\beta_{n}\leq\beta
and
\mathfrak{g}_{n}
is the concatenation of
(\mathfrak{h}_{p})_{p-k^{-}\in Q_{n}}
. By the induction hypothesis,
\mathfrak{g}_{n}\in H
for each
n
. Moreover
(\mathfrak{g}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges towards
\mathfrak{f}_{k}^{-}
so
\mathfrak{f}_{k}^{-}\in H
. By a similar argument
(\mathfrak{f}_{k}^{+})^{-1}\in H
for all
k\in\{k_{i}:0\leq i<m\}
and therefore
\mathfrak{f}_{k}^{+}\in H
for all such
k
.
∎

Proposition 4.31
.

Let
Y\subseteq X
be such that each
y\in Y
has a non-trivial
\mathbb{R}
-stabiliser and
y^{*}\notin\mathbb{R}\cdot y
for all
y\in Y
.
Suppose that
|Y|\leq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
and there exist
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
such that
y_{1}\neq y_{2}
and
y_{1}\notin\mathbb{R}\cdot y_{2}^{*}
. Then
(T_{X}(Y),d)
is the universal
\mathbb{R}
-tree with valence
2^{\aleph_{0}}
.

Proof.

By Lemmas
4.8
and
4.18
,
T_{X}(Y)
is a closed connected subspace of a complete
\mathbb{R}
-tree, so it is itself a complete
\mathbb{R}
-tree. Since the action of
T_{X}(Y)
on itself is transitive, it suffices to check that the valence of
T_{X}(Y)
at
\id
is
2^{\aleph_{0}}
. Let
\kappa
denote the valence of
T_{X}(Y)
at
\id
. Two elements
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}\in T_{X}(Y)
lie in the same connected component of
T_{X}(Y)-\{\id\}
if and only if there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
f(t)=g(t)
for all but countably many
t\in[0,\varepsilon]
. Let
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
,
\ell>0
and let
P=\{p_{n}\in(0,\ell]:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
be a template of
[0,\ell]
such that
p_{n}\rightarrow 0
. For each subset
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{N}
let
x^{\Omega}=(x^{\Omega}_{p_{n}})_{p_{n}\in P}\subseteq X
where
x^{\Omega}_{p_{n}}=y_{1}
if
n\in\Omega
and
x^{\Omega}_{p_{n}}=y_{2}
otherwise. It follows from Lemmas
4.10
and
4.13
, and the assumptions on
y_{1},y_{2}
, that the sequence
x^{\Omega}
and its realisation
\mathfrak{f}_{\Omega}
are admissible. Moreover, if
\Omega,\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and the symmetric difference
\Omega\Delta\Omega^{\prime}
is infinite then
\mathfrak{f}_{\Omega}
and
\mathfrak{f}_{\Omega^{\prime}}
lie in different connected components of
T_{X}(Y)-\{\id\}
. Therefore
\kappa\geq\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}/\sim
, where
\Omega\sim\Omega^{\prime}
if and only if
|\Omega\Delta\Omega^{\prime}|<\infty
. Each equivalence class of subsets has cardinality
\aleph_{0}
so this implies that
\kappa\geq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
. Conversely, Proposition
4.29
implies that cardinality of
T_{X}(Y)
is bounded above by that of
\mathcal{C}\times(\mathbb{R}\cdot(Y\cup Y^{*}))
, where
\mathcal{C}
is the set of countable subsets of
\mathbb{R}
. Thus
\kappa\leq|T_{X}(Y)|\leq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
.
∎

We can now prove that the only lines in
T_{X}(Y)
which can have dense stabilisers are translates of standard axes.

Lemma 4.32
.

Let
Y\subseteq X
be such that each
y\in Y
has a non-trivial
\mathbb{R}
-stabiliser and
y^{*}\notin\mathbb{R}\cdot y
for all
y\in Y
.
Let
L\subseteq T_{X}(Y)
be a line such that
\Stab_{G}(L)
acts on
L
with dense orbits. Then
L=\mathfrak{f}\star L^{\prime}
for some
\mathfrak{f}\in T_{X}(Y)
and some standard axis
L^{\prime}\subseteq T_{X}(Y)
.

Proof.

Up to translating by an element of
T_{X}(Y)
, we can assume that
L
intersects the identity. Let
\mathfrak{f}\in L
be a non-trivial element and let
P\subseteq[0,\ell(\mathfrak{f})]
be a template such that
\mathfrak{f}
is the realisation of a sequence in
X
indexed by
P
. Up to translating by an element of
T_{X}(Y)
, we can assume that
P
can be chosen so that
0\in P
. Let
p\in P
be the successor of
0
and let
x\in Y
be such that
f(t)=x
for all but countably many
t\in[0,p]
. The assumption on the stabiliser subgroup of
L
implies that there exists
\id\precneq g\precneq f
such that
g\in\Stab_{T_{X}(Y)}(L)
. Therefore
\mathfrak{g}\square\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}\in L
, where
f^{\prime}\coloneqq f|_{[0,p]}
. Now
\mathfrak{g}\square\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{h}
where
h([0,\ell(g)])=x
and
h([\ell(\mathfrak{g}),\ell(\mathfrak{g})+p])=-\ell(\mathfrak{g})\cdot x
. But since
\mathfrak{h}\in L
and
\mathfrak{g}\preceq\mathfrak{f}
, we have
\mathfrak{f}\preceq\mathfrak{h}
therefore for all but countably many
t\in[\ell(g),\ell(f)]
we have
-\ell(\mathfrak{g})\cdot x=f(t)=x
. Thus
\mathfrak{h}
is constant with image
x
. It follows by induction that
\mathfrak{g}^{n}\square\mathfrak{f}\in L
is constant with image
x
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular, each
\mathfrak{g}^{n}
is constant with image
x
and
\ell(\mathfrak{g})\in\Stab_{\mathbb{R}}(x)
so it follows that each
\mathfrak{g}^{-n}
is constant with image
x^{*}
. Thus
L=L_{x}\cup L_{x^{*}}
.
∎

4.4
Proof of Theorems
4.1
and
4.2

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

Fix an arbitrary map
\iota:\Sub_{NC}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
. If
\iota
is the zero map then let
X_{0}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\sqcup(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})^{\prime}
, equipped with the natural action of
\mathbb{R}
and the involution
(x+\mathbb{Z})^{*}=(-x+\mathbb{Z})^{\prime}
,
{(x+\mathbb{Z})^{\prime}}^{*}=-x+\mathbb{Z}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. This defines an action of
\mathbb{R}\rtimes_{\alpha}\langle*\rangle
on
X
. Let
G\coloneqq T_{X_{0}}(X_{0})
and recall that, by Proposition
4.31
,
(G,d)
is the complete universal real tree with valence
2^{\aleph_{0}}
. By Lemma
4.32
, all the stabilisers of lines in
T_{X_{0}}(X_{0})
are either trivial or cyclic so the action of
T_{X_{0}}(X_{0})
on itself by left multiplication satisfies the conclusion of the theorem. If
\iota
is the characteristic map of
\mathbb{R}
then let
X_{1}\coloneqq X_{0}\sqcup\{x,x^{\prime}\}
, where
X_{0}
is equipped with the same action of
\mathbb{R}\rtimes_{\alpha}\langle*\rangle
as before,
\mathbb{R}
acts on
\{x,x^{\prime}\}
trivially and
x^{*}\coloneqq x^{\prime},(x^{\prime})^{*}\coloneqq x
. Let
G\coloneqq T_{X_{1}}(X_{1})
and note that
(G,d)
is again the complete universal real tree with valence
2^{\aleph_{0}}
. Then by Lemma
4.32
the only orbit of lines with non-trivial and non-cyclic stabilisers is the orbit of the standard axis
\mathbf{L}_{x}
. It follows that
|A_{H}|=|\{G\cdot\mathbf{L}_{x}\}|=1
if
H=\mathbb{R}
and
|A_{H}|=0
otherwise.
Thus we can (and do) assume that
\iota
is not the zero map or the characteristic map of
\mathbb{R}
.

For each subgroup
H\in\Sub_{NC}(\mathbb{R})
let
B_{H}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}/H\sqcup(\mathbb{R}/H)^{\prime}
and define an action of
\mathbb{R}\rtimes\langle*\rangle
on
B_{H}
as follows. If
r\in\mathbb{R}
and
x+H\in\mathbb{R}/H
then
r\cdot(x+H)\coloneqq r+x+H
,
r\cdot(x+H)^{\prime}=(r+x+H)^{\prime}
, and
(x+H)^{*}\coloneqq(-x+H)^{\prime}
,
{(x+H)^{\prime}}^{*}\coloneqq-x+H
. Let
X_{H}
be the disjoint union of
\iota(H)
copies of
B_{H}
and let
X\coloneqq\sqcup\{X_{H}:H\in\Sub_{NC}(\mathbb{R})\}
. Since
\sum_{H\in\Sub_{NC}(\mathbb{R})}\iota(H)\leq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
, we have
|X|\leq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
.

Let
G\coloneqq T_{X}(X)
. By Proposition
4.31

(G,d)
is the complete universal real tree with valence
2^{\aleph_{0}}
. Let
H\in\Sub_{NC}(\mathbb{R})
. If
L\subseteq G
is a line whose orbit is an element of
A_{H}
then Lemma
4.32
implies that
L=\mathfrak{f}\star L^{\prime}
for some
\mathfrak{f}\in G
and some standard axis
L^{\prime}
. Lemma
4.20
implies that, if
y,y^{\prime}\in Y
and
y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\cdot\{y,y^{*}\}
, then
\mathbf{L}_{y},\mathbf{L}_{y^{\prime}}
are in the same
G
-orbit and it is clear from the definition of the operation on
G
that, if
y^{\prime}\notin\mathbb{R}\cdot\{y,y^{*}\}
, then
\mathbf{L}_{y},\mathbf{L}_{y^{\prime}}
are in different
G
-orbits. By Lemma
4.15
, if
y\in Y
then
G\cdot\mathbf{L}_{y}\in A_{H}
if and only
\Stab_{\mathbb{R}}(y)=H
. Therefore
|A_{H}|
is equal to the number of copies of
B_{H}
in
X_{H}
, which is
\iota(H)
by construction.
∎

To prove Theorem
4.2
we will need two more lemmas:

Lemma 4.33
.

Let
T
be a real tree and let
G
be a group acting freely on
T
. Let
L\subseteq T
be a line such that
\Stab_{G}(L)
acts on
L
with dense orbits. Then, for all
g\in G
, the intersection
gL\cap L
is either empty, a point or a line.

Proof.

Let
M\subseteq T
be a line, let
\varphi:M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be an isometry and let
y\coloneqq\varphi^{-1}(0)
. We will say that the
explicit stabiliser
of
M
is
K\coloneqq\varphi(\Stab_{G}(M)\cdot y)\leq\mathbb{R}
. Note that
K
does not depend on the choice of
\varphi
. It follows from the freeness of the action that, for any
g\in G
and
x\in M
, we have
g\in\Stab_{G}(M)
if and only if
gx\in M
and
d(x,gx)\in K
. Moreover, if
g\in G
then
\varphi\circ g^{-1}:gM\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is an isometry,
(\varphi\circ g^{-1})^{-1}(0)=gy
and
\varphi\circ g^{-1}(\Stab_{G}(gM)\cdot gy)=\varphi(\Stab_{G}(M)\cdot y)=K
, so
K
is an invariant of
G\cdot M
.

Let
H\leq\mathbb{R}
be the explicit stabiliser of
L
.
Let
g\in G
be such that
|gL\cap L|>1
.
The intersection
L\cap gL
is closed and connected so this implies that there is a non-degenerate segment
[x,y]\subseteq L\cap gL
. By assumption, there exists
h\in\Stab_{G}(L)
such that
hx\in[x,y]
.
Then
hx\in gL
and
d(x,gx)\in H
so
h\in\Stab_{G}(L)\cap\Stab_{G}(gL)
. If
z\in[x,y]
is such that
d(z,y)<d(x,hx)
then
hz\in L\cap gL
and
hz\notin[x,y]
. This argument applies to any non-degenerate segment
[x,y]\subseteq L\cap gL
, so
L\cap gL
has infinite length. Since the action of
G
is free,
L\cap gL
cannot be a ray, so we must have that
L\cap gL=L
.
∎

Given a group
G
acting freely and transitively on an
\mathbb{R}
-tree
T
, we establish the following convention. Identify
G
with
T
via an orbit map
g\mapsto g\cdot x_{0}
for some
x_{0}\in T
and equip
G
with the partial order defined by
g\preceq f
if
[\id,g]\subseteq[\id,f]
. Note that
(G,\preceq)
is a meet semilattice and
g\wedge f\neq\id
if and only if
f
and
g
are in the same direction at
\id
. For all
g\in G
define
\ell(g)\coloneqq d(\id,g)
, where
d
is the metric on
T
.

Lemma 4.34
.

Let
\kappa\leq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
be a cardinal. Let
T
be a real tree which is not a single point and such that there is a group
G
acting freely and transitively on
T
. If, for any line
L\subseteq T
, we have
|\Stab_{G}(L)\setminus L|\geq\kappa
, then the valence of
T
is
\geq\kappa
.

Proof.

We first assume that the
G
-stabiliser of every line in
T
is at most countable.

Claim 1
.

Let
f\in G
be non-trivial and, for each
g\prec f
, let
h_{g}\coloneqq g^{-1}f\wedge f
. Then, for all but countably many
g\prec f
, we have
h_{g}=\id
.

Proof.

Let
A\subseteq G
be the set of
g\prec f
such that
h_{g}\neq\id
and suppose that
A
is uncountable. For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
A_{n}\coloneqq\{g\in A:\ell(h_{g})\geq 1/n\}
. Then
A=\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}A_{n}
so there is some
n
for which
A_{n}
is uncountable. Since
[\id,f]
is a finite union of segments of length
\leq 1/2n
, there exists a segment
I\subseteq[\id,f]
with length
\leq 1/2n
such that
P\coloneqq A_{n}\cap I
is uncountable. We can moreover assume, up to taking a subsegment with the same property, that
I=[p_{1},p_{2}]
for some
p_{1},p_{2}\in[\id,f]
such that
p_{1},p_{2}\in A_{n}
.
Let
k\coloneqq p_{1}^{-1}p_{2}
. Then
k\preceq h_{p}
for all
p\in P
so
pk\preceq f
. In particular,
p_{1}k^{2}\preceq f
and
p_{1}k\preceq f
which implies that
k\wedge k^{-1}=\id
. Let
L\subseteq G
be the convex hull of
\langle k\rangle
and note that
L
is a line. For every
p\in P
we have
p_{1}\preceq pk\preceq p_{1}k^{2}
and it follows that
p_{1}^{-1}p
stabilises
L
, but there are uncountably many such elements.

\blacksquare

Let
G_{f}\coloneqq\{g\in G:g\preceq f\}
and define an equivalence relation
\sim
on
G_{f}
by:
g\sim g^{\prime}
if
g^{-1}f\wedge{g^{\prime}}^{-1}f\neq\id
. If
g\sim g^{\prime}
and
g\prec g^{\prime}\prec f
then
g^{-1}g^{\prime}\preceq g^{-1}f
and
(g^{-1}g^{\prime})^{-1}g^{-1}f\wedge g^{-1}f={g^{\prime}}^{-1}f\wedge g^{-1}f\neq\id
. By Claim
1
, there are at most countably many such elements
g^{-1}g^{\prime}\preceq g^{-1}f
. It follows that equivalence classes of
G_{f}
are at most countable, which implies that
|G_{f}/\sim|=2^{\aleph_{0}}
, and thus the valence of
T
is
\geq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
.

Now assume
T
has valence
<2^{\aleph_{0}}
. By the above argument, there is a line
L\subseteq T
whose stabiliser is uncountable. Up to translating
L
by some element of
G
, we can assume that
\id\in L
. The orbit
\Stab_{G}(L)\cdot x
is dense in
L
for any
x\in L
so, by Lemma
4.33
, for any
f,g\in G
, the intersection
fL\cap gL
is either empty, a single point or a line. Therefore, if
f,g\in L
and
f^{-1}\wedge g^{-1}\neq\id
, then
f^{-1}L=g^{-1}L
so
fg^{-1}\in\Stab_{G}(L)
. By assumption
|\Stab_{G}(L)\setminus L|\geq\kappa
so this implies that the valence of
T
is bounded below by
\kappa
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

Let
\kappa\geq 4
be a cardinal which is either infinite or even. Let
I_{\kappa}
be a set with cardinality
\kappa/2
and let
X_{\kappa}\coloneqq\{x_{i},x_{i}^{*}:i\in I_{\kappa}\}
, equipped with the trivial action of
\mathbb{R}
and the involution
\ast:x_{i}\mapsto x_{i}^{*},x_{i}^{*}\mapsto x_{i}
. Let
H_{\kappa}\leq T_{X_{\kappa}}(X_{\kappa})
be the subgroup of complexity 1 elements of
T_{X_{\kappa}}(X_{\kappa})
. Then
H_{\kappa}
is an incomplete real tree and the set of directions at
\id
is in bijection with
X
.

Next, fix a cardinal
3\leq\kappa<2^{\aleph_{0}}
and suppose there exists a real tree
T
with valence
\kappa
and a group
G
which admits a free transitive action on
T
.

Suppose
T
is complete. We will show that
\kappa\geq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
, leading to a contradiction. We will need to construct a set
\{a_{1,n},a_{2,n}\in G:n\in\mathbb{R}\}
such that
\ell(a_{i,n})\leq 1/n^{2}
for all
n
and, for all
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
a_{1,n}^{-1}\wedge a_{1,m}=a_{1,n}^{-1}\wedge a_{2,m}=a_{2,n}\wedge a_{2,m}^{-1}=\id
.
To this end, we first show that there exist lines
L_{1},L_{2}\subseteq T
such that
L_{1}\cap L_{2}=\{\id\}
and the actions
\Stab_{G}(L_{i})\curvearrowright L_{i}
have dense orbits.

By Lemma
4.34
, there exists a line
L_{1}\subseteq T
with uncountable, and therefore dense, stabiliser. Up to translating by an element of
G
, we can assume that
\id\in L_{1}
.
If the stabiliser of
L_{1}
is not transitive, let
g\in L_{1}-\Stab_{G}(L_{1})
and
L_{2}\coloneqq g^{-1}L_{1}
. Then
L_{2}\neq L_{1}
and
\id\in L_{2}
so by Lemma
4.33

L_{1}\cap L_{2}=\{\id\}
.
If
\Stab_{G}(L_{1})
is transitive then
\Stab_{G}(L_{1})=L_{1}
. Let
H\subseteq G
be the set of elements
g\in G
such that the segment
[\id,g]
does not intersect any translate of
L_{1}
in a non-degenerate segment. If
g,h\in H
, then
[\id,gh]=[\id,g^{\prime}]\cup g^{\prime}\cdot[h^{\prime},h]
for some
g^{\prime}\preceq g
and
h^{\prime}\preceq h
. For all
k\in G
we have
[\id,g^{\prime}]\cap kL_{1}\subseteq[\id,g]\cap kL_{1}
and
g^{\prime}\cdot[h^{\prime},h]\cap kL_{1}\subseteq g^{\prime}\cdot([\id,h]\cap{g^{\prime}}^{-1}kL_{1})
so
|[\id,gh]\cap kL_{1}|\leq 1
and
gh\in H
. Also
[\id,g^{-1}]\cap kL_{1}=g^{-1}\cdot([\id,g]\cap gkL_{1})
which has cardinality at most 1 so
g^{-1}\in H
. Thus
H
is a subgroup of
G
. It is clear from the definition that
H
is a subtree of
G
. It follows from the fact that
\Stab_{G}(L_{1})=L_{1}
that
H
is non-trivial – more precisely, the valence of
H
is
\kappa-2
. Thus by Lemma
4.34
there is a line
L_{2}\subseteq H
containing
\id
such that
\Stab_{H}(L_{2})=\Stab_{G}(L_{2})
acts on
L_{2}
with dense stabilisers.

Now fix rays
L_{i}^{+}\subseteq L_{i}
based at
\id
for each
i
.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
a_{1,n}\in L_{1}^{+}\cap\Stab_{G}(L_{1}),a_{2,n}\in L_{2}^{+}\cap\Stab_{G}(L_{2})
be such that
0<\ell(a_{1,n})<\ell(a_{2,n})\leq 1/n^{2}
. Then for all
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
a_{1,n}^{-1},a_{1,m}^{-1}\in L_{1}-L_{1}^{+}
and
a_{2,n}^{-1},a_{2,m}^{-1}\in L_{2}-L_{2}^{+}
so

a_{1,n}^{-1}\wedge a_{1,m}=a_{2,n}^{-1}\wedge a_{2,m}^{-1}=a_{1,n}^{-1}\wedge a_{2,m}=a_{2,n}^{-1}\wedge a_{1,m}=\id.

Given a map
\theta:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\{1,2\}
, define
g^{\theta}_{n}\coloneqq a_{\theta(1),1}\dots a_{\theta(n),n}
. The way we chose the
a_{1,i}
’s and
a_{2,i}
’s implies that
\ell(g^{\theta}_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(a_{\theta(i),i})
,
g^{\theta}_{n}\prec g^{\theta}_{n+1}
and
(\ell(g^{\theta}_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is convergent, so
(g^{\theta}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy and has a limit
g^{\theta}\in G
. It also follows from the choice of
a_{1,i}
’s and
a_{2,i}
’s that, if
\theta\neq\theta^{\prime}
, then
(g^{\theta})^{-1}\wedge(g^{\theta^{\prime}})^{-1}=\id
which means that
T
has at least
2^{\aleph_{0}}
directions at
\id
.

Finally, suppose that
\kappa
is finite. By Lemma
4.34
, there is a line
L\subseteq T
such that
|\Stab_{G}(L)\setminus L|\leq\kappa
. The additive group
\mathbb{R}
has no finite index subgroups, so this implies that
\Stab_{G}(L)
acts transitively on
L
. We assume without loss of generality that
\id\in L
and note that
L=\Stab_{G}(L)
in this case. Let
H\subseteq G
be the set of elements
g\in G
such that the segment
[\id,g]
does not intersect any translate of
L_{1}
in a non-degenerate segment. Recall that
H
is a subgroup of
G
and a subtree with valence
\kappa-2
. It follows by induction on
\kappa
that
\kappa
is even and there are lines
L_{1},\dots,L_{\kappa/2}\subseteq T
such that
L_{i}\cap L_{j}=\{\id\}
for all
i\neq j
and
\Stab_{G}(L_{i})=L_{i}
for all
i
.

To prove the uniqueness statement, recall the definition of the group
H_{\kappa}
from the first paragraph of this proof. For each
i\in\{1,\dots,\kappa/2\}
fix an isometry
\psi:\mathbf{L}_{x_{i}}\rightarrow L_{i}
such that
\psi_{i}(\id)=\id
. These extend to an isometric and homomorphic embedding
\psi:H_{\kappa}\rightarrow T
.

It remains to show that
\psi
is surjective. Let
p\in T
be in the closure of
\psi(H_{\kappa})
and let
(h_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\psi(H_{\kappa})
be a sequence with limit
p
. Since
\psi(H_{\kappa})
is connected, we can assume without loss of generality that
h_{n}\preceq h_{n+1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Each
p^{-1}h_{n}
lies in the same direction at
\id
and
\ell(p^{-1}h_{n})\rightarrow 0
. Therefore there exists
i\in\{1,\dots,\kappa/2\}
such that , for sufficiently large
n
, we have
p^{-1}h_{n}\in L_{i}\subseteq\psi(H_{\kappa})
. Thus
p\in\psi(H_{\kappa})
and
\psi(H_{\kappa})
is closed. Now suppose there exists
p\in T-\psi(H_{\kappa})
and let
p^{\prime}\in\psi(H_{\kappa})
be the closest point projection of
p
onto
\psi(H_{\kappa})
. Then
[p^{\prime},p]\cap\psi(H_{\kappa})=\{p^{\prime}\}
, so
p
lies in a different direction at
p^{\prime}
to any point of
\psi(H_{\kappa})
. But since the valence of
H_{\kappa}
is
\kappa
, this implies that the valence of
p^{\prime}
in
T
is
\geq\kappa+1
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Remark 4.35
.

Let
\kappa\geq 4
be a cardinal which is not both finite and odd, and let
H_{\kappa}
be the group defined at the start of the above proof. Then
H_{\kappa}
is isomorphic to the free product of
\kappa
copies of
\mathbb{R}
and the action of
H_{\kappa}
on itself is precisely the action constructed in
[
CM12
]
for free products of copies of
\mathbb{R}
.

For each
i\in I_{\kappa}
, let
R_{i}=\mathbb{R}
. Let
\ast_{I_{\kappa}}R_{i}
be the free product of
\{R_{i}:i\in I_{\kappa}\}
. Recall that, for each
i\in I_{\kappa}
, since
\Stab_{H_{\kappa}}(\mathbf{L}_{x_{i}})=\mathbf{L}_{x_{i}}
, the map
\mathbf{L}_{x_{i}}\rightarrow R_{i}
which maps
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathbf{L}_{x_{i}}
to
\ell(\mathfrak{f})
if the image of
\mathfrak{f}
is
x_{i}
, and maps
\mathfrak{f}
to
-\ell(\mathfrak{f})
otherwise, is an isomorphism. The group
H_{\kappa}
is generated by
\cup_{i\in I_{\kappa}}\mathbf{L}_{x_{i}}
, so these isomorphisms extend to an isomorphism
\psi:H_{\kappa}\rightarrow\ast_{I_{\kappa}}R_{i}
. Moreover, length function
\ell\circ\psi^{-1}:\ast_{I_{\kappa}}R_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is precisely the Lyndon length function defined in
[
CM12
]
to construct a free transitive action on an
\mathbb{R}
-tree. It follows that the resulting free transitive actions are isomorphic.

If
\kappa=2^{\aleph_{0}}
then
H_{\kappa}\curvearrowright H_{\kappa}
is also isomorphic to the free transitive action on Uryson’s tree constructed in
[
Ber89
,
Ber19
]
.

4.5
Reduction retraction

x^{*}\notin\mathbb{R}\cdot x
and
\Stab_{\mathbb{R}}(x)
is non-trivial for all
x\in X
.

for all
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{Z}_{X}
.

Let us start with some terminology.

Definition 4.37

(Reducible at
t
)
.

Let
\ell>0
and
Q\subseteq[0,\ell]
be a finite template. Let
(\mathfrak{f}_{q})_{q\in Q}\subseteq T_{X}
be a consistent sequence with concatenation
\mathfrak{f}
. We say that
\mathfrak{f}
is
reducible at

t
if there exists
t_{1},t_{2}\in Q
with
t_{1}<t<t_{2}
such that
Q\cap(t_{1},t_{2})=\{t\}
and there exists
0<s\leq\min\{t-t_{1},t_{2}-t\}
such that for all but countably many
0\leq\varepsilon\leq s
we have
f_{t_{1}}^{-1}(\varepsilon)=f_{t}(\varepsilon)
.

Remark 4.38
.

If
\mathfrak{f}
is the concatenation of another finite sequence
(\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}})_{q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}}\subseteq T_{X}
then
t\in Q^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{f}
is also reducible at
t
with respect to
(\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}_{q^{\prime}})_{q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}}
. Thus reducibility at
t
is well-defined.

We now define reduction of a finite sequence of elements of
T_{X}
.

Definition 4.39

(Reduction of a finite sequence at
t
)
.

Let
\ell>0
and
Q\subseteq[0,\ell]
be a finite template. Let
(\mathfrak{f}_{q})_{q\in Q}\subseteq T_{X}
be a consistent but inadmissible sequence with concatenation
\mathfrak{f}
and suppose that
\mathfrak{f}
is reducible at
t\in Q
. Let
t_{1}
be the predecessor of
t
and
t_{2}
be its successor. Let
\sigma>0
be the maximal
0<s\leq\min\{t-t_{1},t_{2}-t\}
such that
f_{t_{1}}^{*}(t-t_{1}-\varepsilon)=2t\cdot f_{t}(\varepsilon)
for all but countably many
0\leq\varepsilon\leq s
. Let
Q^{t}\coloneqq Q\cup\{t-\sigma,t+\sigma\}
and define a sequence
(\mathfrak{f}^{t}_{q})_{q\in Q^{t}}\subseteq T_{X}
as follows:

•

if
q<t_{1}
or
q\geq t_{2}
, then
f_{q}^{t}\coloneqq f_{q}
;

•

if
t_{1}\neq t-\sigma
, then
f^{t}_{t_{1}}\coloneqq f_{t_{1}}|_{[0,t-t_{1}-\sigma]}
;

•

f^{t}_{t-\sigma}:[0,\sigma]\rightarrow X
is defined by
f^{t}_{t-\sigma}(u)=(t-\sigma)\cdot f_{t_{1}}(u+t-t_{1}-\sigma)
;

•

f^{t}_{t}\coloneqq f_{t}|_{[0,\sigma]}
;

•

if
t+\sigma\neq t_{2}
, then
f^{t}_{t+\sigma}:[0,t_{2}-t-\sigma]\rightarrow X
is defined by
f^{t}_{t+\sigma}(u)=\sigma\cdot f_{t}(u+\sigma)
.

Observe that the concatenation of
(\mathfrak{f}^{t}_{q})_{q\in Q^{t}}
is
\mathfrak{f}
.
Now define

Q_{t}\coloneqq\{q\in Q^{t}:q\leq t-\sigma\}\cup\{q-2\sigma:q\in Q^{t},q\geq t+\sigma\}

and note that
Q_{t}
is a finite template of
[0,\ell-2\sigma]
. Define
r_{Q,t}:Q^{t}-\{t\}\rightarrow Q_{t}
by
r_{Q,t}(q)=q
if
q\leq t-\sigma
and
r_{Q,t}(q)=q-2\sigma
otherwise.

Given
q\in Q_{t}
let
\mathfrak{f}^{(t)}_{q}\coloneqq\mathfrak{f}^{t}_{q}
if
q<t-\sigma
and
\mathfrak{f}^{(t)}_{q}=\mathfrak{f}^{t}_{q+2\sigma}
otherwise. The
reduction

\mathrm{red}(\mathfrak{f},Q,t)
of
(\mathfrak{f},Q)
at
t
is the concatenation of
(\mathfrak{f}^{(t)}_{q})_{q\in Q_{t}}
.

Remark 4.40
.

By the maximality of
\sigma
, if
t-\sigma\neq t_{1}
and
t+\sigma\neq t_{2}
then
\mathfrak{f}^{(t)}
is not reducible at
t-\sigma
. This implies that the number of points at which
\mathrm{red}(\mathfrak{f},Q,t)
is reducible is strictly smaller than the number of points at which
\mathfrak{f}
is reducible.

Lemma 4.41
.

Suppose that
\ell,Q,(\mathfrak{f}_{q})_{q\in Q}
and
\mathfrak{f}
are as in Definition
4.39
and that
\mathfrak{f}
is reducible at the points
s,t\in Q
. Let
U
be the template and
(\mathfrak{g}_{u})_{u\in U}
be the sequence obtained by first reducing
\mathfrak{f}
at
s
then at
r_{Q,s}(t)
and let
V
be the template and
(\mathfrak{h}_{v})_{v\in V}
be the sequence obtained by reducing
\mathfrak{f}
first at
t
then at
r_{Q,t}(s)
. Then
U=V
and
(\mathfrak{g}_{u})_{u\in U}=(\mathfrak{h}_{v})_{v\in V}
.

Proof.

Relabel
s
and
t
if necessary so that
s<t
and let
s_{1},s_{2}\in Q^{s}
,
t_{1},t_{2}\in Q^{t}
be such that
s_{1}<s_{2}
,
t_{1}<t_{2}
,
Q^{s}\cap(s_{1},s_{2})=\{s\}
and
Q^{t}\cap(t_{1},t_{2})=\{t\}
. Let
\sigma\coloneqq s-s_{1}=s_{2}-s
and
\tau\coloneqq t-t_{1}=t_{2}-t
. First suppose that
s_{2}\leq t_{1}
. It is clear from the definition that the reduction of
x
at
s
is reducible at
r_{Q,s}(t)
and similarly the reduction of
x
at
t
is reducible at
r_{Q,t}(s)
.
Since the collapsed intervals are disjoint, it is clear that
U=V
and
(\mathfrak{g}_{u})_{u\in U}=(\mathfrak{h}_{v})_{v\in V}
in this case.

So suppose that
t_{1}<s_{2}
. In this case
s_{1}\leq s\leq t_{1}<s_{2}\leq t\leq t_{2}
.
Let
\sigma^{\prime}\coloneqq t_{1}-s
and
\tau^{\prime}\coloneqq t-s_{2}
. Then

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=\{p\in Q:p\leq s_{1}\}\cup\{p-2(\sigma+\tau^{\prime}):p\in Q,p\geq t_{2}\}

\displaystyle=\{p\in Q:p\leq s_{1}\}\cup\{p-2(t-s):p\in Q,p\geq t_{2}\}

and

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\{p\in Q:p\leq s-\sigma^{\prime}\}\cup\{p-2(\tau+\sigma^{\prime}):p\in Q,p\geq t_{2}\}

\displaystyle=\{p\in Q:p\leq s-\sigma^{\prime}\}\cup\{p-2(t-s):p\in Q,p\geq t_{2}\}.

Since
t_{2}-2(\sigma+\tau^{\prime})=s-\sigma^{\prime}
, we indeed have
U=V
.

Let
s_{1}^{\prime}\in Q
be such that
s_{1}^{\prime}<s
and
Q\cap(s_{1}^{\prime},s)=\emptyset
and let
t_{2}^{\prime}\in Q
be such that
t<t_{2}^{\prime}
and
Q\cap(t,t_{2}^{\prime})=\emptyset
. For each
p<s_{1}^{\prime}
,
g_{p}=f_{p}=h_{p}
. Also if
s_{1}\neq s_{1}^{\prime}
then
g_{s_{1}^{\prime}}=(f_{s_{1}^{\prime}})|_{[0,s_{1}-s_{1}^{\prime}]}=h_{s_{1}^{\prime}}
.
We have
g_{s_{1}}:[0,\tau-\tau^{\prime}]\rightarrow X
is given by
g_{s_{1}}(r)=(t+\tau^{\prime})\cdot f(r+t+\tau^{\prime})
and
h_{s_{1}}:[0,\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}]\rightarrow X
is given by
h_{s_{1}}(r)=s_{1}\cdot f(r+s_{1})
.
Note that
\tau-\tau^{\prime}=\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}
and
t
is the successor of
s
in
Q
. By assumption,
(\mathfrak{f}^{s}_{s_{1}})^{-1}=\mathfrak{f}_{s}^{s}
and
(\mathfrak{f}^{t}_{t_{1}})^{-1}=\mathfrak{f}_{t}^{t}
. Therefore, for all
r\in[0,\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}]
,

s_{1}\cdot f(r+s_{1})=f^{s}_{s_{1}}(r)=(f^{s}_{s})^{-1}(r)=-\sigma\cdot(s\cdot f(s+(\sigma-r)))^{*}=-s_{2}\cdot f^{*}(s_{2}-r)

and

\displaystyle(t+\tau^{\prime})\cdot f(r+t+\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau^{\prime}\cdot f_{t}^{t}(r+\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\tau^{\prime}\cdot(f_{t_{1}}^{t})^{-1}(r+\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)\cdot(t_{1}\cdot f(t-(r+\tau^{\prime})))^{*}

\displaystyle=-s_{2}\cdot f^{*}(s_{2}-r)

Lastly, for all
p\in Q
with
p\geq t_{2}^{\prime}
, we have
g_{p-2(t-s)}=f_{p}=h_{p-2(t-s)}
and, if
t_{2}\neq t_{2}^{\prime}
, then
g_{t_{2}},h_{t_{2}}:[0,t_{2}^{\prime}-t_{2}]\rightarrow X
are defined by
g_{t_{2}}(r)=h_{t_{2}}(r)=f_{t}(r+\tau)
.
∎

Combining Lemma
4.41
with Remark
4.40
, the following is well-defined.

Definition 4.42

.

, let

. If

. If

then reduce it at

. Iterating this procedure, we eventually obtain an admissible element

In the case where

, Property (C) holds by Lemma
4.43
and Property (F) holds by Lemma
4.41
. To check Property (I) in this case, recall that
\mathfrak{f}^{-1}
is the realisation of the sequence
(-\ell\cdot y_{p}^{*})_{p\in P^{-1}}
, where
P^{-1}\coloneqq\{\ell-p:p\in P\}
and
y_{p}=-\ell\cdot x_{p^{\prime}}^{*}
where
p^{\prime}\in P
is the predecessor of
\ell-p\in P
. Property (I) therefore follows from Definition
4.42
.

Now let
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{Z}_{X}
be an element with complexity
\alpha
. Let
\ell\coloneqq\ell(\mathfrak{f})
, let
P\subseteq[0,\ell]
be a template and
(x_{p})_{p\in P}\subseteq X
be a sequence with realisation
\mathfrak{f}
. Suppose moreover that
\overline{P}^{(\beta)}\subseteq\overline{Q}^{(\beta)}
for any template
Q
which indexes a sequence in
X
with realisation
\mathfrak{f}
. Note that this property renders
\overline{P}^{(\beta)}
unique (although
P
itself may not be). Since the complexity of
\mathfrak{f}
is
\alpha
,
\overline{P}^{(\beta)}
is finite.

Proof.

If the complexities of both
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{g}
are strictly less than
\alpha
, then the claim follows by the induction hypothesis. So suppose that at least one of
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}
has complexity
\alpha
.

Let
\ell\coloneqq\ell(\mathfrak{f}),m\coloneqq\ell(g)
and let
P\subseteq[0,\ell],Q\in[0,m]
be templates which can index sequences whose realisations are
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{g}
respectively. Suppose moreover that
\overline{P}^{(\beta)}\subseteq\overline{P^{\prime}}^{(\beta)}
for any template
P^{\prime}
which indexes a sequence in
X
with realisation
\mathfrak{f}
, and similarly
\overline{Q}^{(\beta)}\subseteq\overline{Q^{\prime}}^{(\beta)}
for any template
Q^{\prime}
which indexes a sequence in
X
with realisation
\mathfrak{g}
.

\hbox to0pt{\vbox to0pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@beveljoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
