The first and last inequalities follow from the definition of reduction and the second follows from Lemma
4.41
.
∎

This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

5
Actions on
\Lambda
-trees

Some of the ideas from the previous section can also be used to construct actions on
\Lambda
-trees for arbitrary totally ordered abelian groups
\Lambda
. Since we do not assume that
\Lambda
is uncountable or that every bounded set in
\Lambda
has a supremum, the exact analogue of the construction does not work. On the other hand, the notion of completeness is no longer very relevant in this context so there is no need for the more involved notion of templates introduced in Section
4.3
to ensure completeness. The group we obtain will be an analogue of the subgroup of
T_{X}(X)
consisting of elements with complexity 1.

As before, the construction allows for flexibility with respect to the stabilisers of ‘lines’. To illustrate this, we will prove the following proposition.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\Lambda
be a totally ordered abelian group, let
H\leq\Lambda
be any subgroup and let
\kappa=2|\Lambda/H|
. Then there exists a group
G
acting freely and transitively on a
\Lambda
-tree
T
with valence
\kappa
such that there exists a subspace
L\subseteq T
and isometry
\varphi:L\rightarrow\Lambda
(where
\Lambda
is equipped with the
\Lambda
-metric
d(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})=|\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}|
) such that
\varphi(\Stab_{G}(L)\cdot x)=H
, where
x=\varphi^{-1}(0)
. In particular
\Stab_{G}(L)\cong H
.

Let
\alpha:\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda
be the order reversing automorphism of
\Lambda
defined by
\alpha(\lambda)=-\lambda
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
.
Let
X
be a set equipped with an action of
\Lambda\rtimes_{\alpha}\langle*\rangle
, where
*
is an element of order two. We will abuse notation and identify
\Lambda
with the normal subgroup
(\Lambda,\id)\unlhd\Lambda\rtimes_{\alpha}\langle\ast\rangle
.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}_{X}
denote the set of maps
f:(0,\ell]\rightarrow X
, where
\ell\in\Lambda
with
\ell\geq 0
, such that, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and some finite sequence
0=p_{0}<\dots<p_{k}=\ell
, the map
f
if constant on each interval
(p_{i},p_{i+1}]
.
When
\ell=0
,
f
is necessarily the empty map, which we denote by
\id:\emptyset\rightarrow X
.
The
length
of
f:(0,\ell]\rightarrow X
is
\ell(f)\coloneqq\ell
.

Remark 5.3
.

We can define templates for intervals in
\Lambda
in analogy with the notion for intervals in
\mathbb{R}
(see Definition
4.21
). Then the set
P\coloneqq\{p_{0},\dots,p_{k}\}
in the above definition is just a finite template for the interval
[0,\ell]
and
f
is the realisation of a sequence
(x_{p})_{p\in P}\subseteq X
.

Definition 5.4
.

Fix
f,g\in\mathcal{Y}_{X}
.

•

Define
f^{-1}:(0,\ell(f)]\rightarrow X
as follows. Let
\{p_{0},\dots,p_{k}\}\subseteq[0,\ell]
be a template for
f
and for each
i\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
and
t\in(p_{i},p_{i+1}]
let
f^{-1}(t)=-\ell(f)\cdot f^{*}(p_{i+1})
.

•

Define
f\square g:(0,\ell(f)+\ell(g)]\rightarrow X
by

f\square g(t)=\begin{cases}f(t)\quad&\text{if }t\in(0,\ell(f)];\\
-\ell(f)\cdot g(t-\ell(f))&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

•

We say that
f\preceq g
if
f
is a restriction of
g
.

Lemma 5.5
.

(\mathcal{Y}_{X},\preceq,\id,\square)
is an ore.

Proof.

It is straightforward to see that
(Y,\preceq,\id)
is a median semilattice. The rest of the proof is completely analogous to that of Lemma
4.7
.
∎

Let
(S_{X},\star)
be the group extracted from
\mathcal{Y}_{X}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

The map
\ell:\mathcal{Y}_{X}\rightarrow\Lambda
is a
\Lambda
-length function (Definition
3.28
). If
d
is the resulting
\Lambda
-metric on
S_{X}
then
(S_{X},d)
is a
\Lambda
-tree.

If
x
and
x^{*}
are in different
\Lambda
-orbits for all
x\in X
then the valence of
S_{X}
is the cardinality of
X
.

Proof.

It is clear from the definition that
\ell
is a
\Lambda
-length function. By Proposition
3.29

(S_{X},d)
is a median
\Lambda
-metric space. For any
f\in S_{X}-\{\id\}
we have
f^{\perp}=\{\id\}
so
S_{X}
has rank 1 by Lemma
3.31
. For any
f\in S_{X}
, the map
t\in[0,\ell(f)]\mapsto f|_{(0,t]}\in S_{X}
is a geodesic from
\id
to
f
. Therefore
S_{X}
is a
\Lambda
-tree by Lemma
2.11
.

Fix an element
\lambda\in\Lambda
with
\lambda>0
.
If
x
and
x^{*}
are in different
\Lambda
-orbits then the constant map
f_{x}:(0,\lambda]\rightarrow X
with image
x
is admissible (by a similar argument to the proof of Lemma
4.13
). Assume that
x
and
x^{*}
are in different
\Lambda
-orbits for all
x\in X
. For all
x\neq y
we have
f_{x}\wedge f_{y}=\id
and for any
g\in S_{X}
there is some
x
such that
f_{x}\wedge g>\id
, so the set of directions at
\id
is in bijection with
X
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Let
X\coloneqq(\Lambda/H)\sqcup(\Lambda/H)^{\prime}
equipped with the obvious
\Lambda
-action and define
*:X\rightarrow X
by
(\lambda+H)^{*}=(-\lambda+H)^{\prime}
and
{(\lambda+H)^{\prime}}^{*}=-\lambda+H
for all
x+H\in\Lambda/H
. This defines an action of
\Lambda\rtimes_{\alpha}\langle*\rangle
on
X
. Let
(S_{X},\star)
be the resulting group, equipped with its canonical
\Lambda
-metric
d
. Then
S_{X}
is a
\Lambda
-tree with valence
|X|=2|\Lambda/H|
. Moreover, for each
x\in X
, there is subspace
\mathbf{L}_{x}\coloneqq\{f\in S_{X}:f
is constant with image
x
or
x^{*}\}
and the map
\varphi:\mathbf{L}_{x}\rightarrow\Lambda
, defined by
\varphi(f)=\ell(f)
if the image of
f
is
x
and
\varphi(f)=-\ell(f)
otherwise, is an isometry. Then,
\varphi(\Stab_{S_{X}}(\mathbf{L}_{x})\cdot\id)=H
so this completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.7

(2-torsion)
.

If
\Lambda
is not 2-divisible then
S_{X}
can contain some order 2 elements which act by inversions (i.e. fixed point free order two isometries). Suppose that
\lambda\in\Lambda
is such that
2\lambda^{\prime}\neq\lambda
for all
\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda
and let
X\coloneqq\Lambda/\langle\lambda\rangle
, equipped with the natural action of
\Lambda
and the involution
(t+\langle\lambda\rangle)^{*}=-t+\langle\lambda\rangle
. Let
S_{X}
be the resulting group and
\Lambda
-tree. Let
f:(0,\lambda]\rightarrow X
be the constant map with image
x\coloneq 0+\langle\lambda\rangle
. If
f=a\square b\square b^{-1}\square c
then
2\ell(b)\in\langle\lambda\rangle
but since
0\leq\ell(b)<\lambda
and
\lambda
is not 2-divisible this can only happen if
b=\id
. So
f\in S_{X}
and
f^{-1}
is constant with image
-\lambda\cdot x=x
, so
f^{-1}=f
.

The only fixed point free isometries of
\Lambda
-trees are inversions and hyperbolic isometries (which have infinite order) so the only finite order elements in any
S_{X}
have order 2. Moreover
S_{X}
can only contain inversions if
\Lambda
is not 2-divisible
[
Chi01
, Lemma 3.1.2, Theorem 5.1.4]
.

6
Actions on products of
\mathbb{R}
-trees

In this section, we present two distinct constructions of free transitive actions on
\ell^{1}
products of
\mathbb{R}
-trees. In Subsection
6.1
, we extend the ideas of Subsection
4.1
to produce groups which act on products of
\mathbb{R}
-trees with arbitrary flat stabilisers. To illustrate the flexibility of this construction, we will prove a variant of Theorem
4.1
(Theorem
6.1
). After establishing some facts about reducible actions on products of
\mathbb{R}
-trees in Subsection
6.2
, we will use the construction from Subsection
6.1
to prove the existence of a free transitive and irreducible action on a product of two
\mathbb{R}
-trees in Subsection
6.3
. In Subsection
6.4
, we present an entirely different construction which allows one to isometrically embed any BMW group with a positive BMW presentation into a free dense action on a product of two
\mathbb{R}
-trees.

6.1
Actions with prescribed flat stabilisers

Let
N\in\{\mathbb{N}\}\cup\{\{1,\dots,n\}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
, let
\mathbf{R}\coloneqq\ell^{1}(N)
be equipped with its natural additive group structure, and, for each
n\in N
, let
\chi_{n}\in\mathbf{R}
be the characteristic map of
n
.

Recall that
\mathcal{K}
is the set of cardinals
\kappa
such that
\kappa\leq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
and let
\Sub_{D}(\mathbf{R})
be the set of dense subgroups
H\leq\mathbf{R}
. Let
\overline{\Sub}_{D}(\mathbf{R})
be the quotient of
\Sub_{D}(\mathbf{R})
under linear isometries and denote the equivalence class of each
H\in\Sub_{D}(\mathbb{R})
by
[H]
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\iota:\overline{\Sub}_{D}(\mathbf{R})\rightarrow\mathcal{K}
and
\eta:N\rightarrow\{0,1\}
be arbitrary maps such that
\eta
is non-zero and
\iota
is supported on
\leq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
elements of
\overline{\Sub}_{D}(\mathbf{R})
.
For each
n\in N
such that
\eta(n)=1
, let
T_{n}
be the universal real tree with valence
2^{\aleph_{0}}
and, for all
n\in N
such that
\eta(n)=0
, let
T_{n}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}
. Let
\mathcal{T}\coloneqq(T_{n})_{n\in N}
.
Then there exists a group
G
which acts freely and transitively on the
\ell^{1}
product
\ell^{1}(\mathcal{T},z)
, for some
z\in\prod_{n\in N}T_{n}
, such that the following holds. For each
[H]\in\overline{\Sub}_{D}(\mathbf{R})
, let
A_{H}
be the set of
G
-orbits of maximal flats
F\subseteq S
such that
\Stab_{G}(F)\curvearrowright F
is isomorphic to
H\curvearrowright\mathbf{R}
.
Then
|A_{H}|=\iota([H])
.

For each
n\in N
, let
X_{n}
be a set equipped with an action of
\mathbf{R}
and let
Y_{n}\coloneqq X_{n}\sqcup X_{n}^{*}
, where
X_{n}^{*}
is a copy of
X_{n}
and
*:X_{n}\rightarrow X_{n}^{*}
is a bijection. For each
x^{*}\in X_{n}^{*}
, let
(x_{n}^{*})^{*}\coloneqq x_{n}
, so
\ast:Y_{n}\rightarrow Y_{n}
is an involution. For each
x_{n}^{*}\in X_{n}^{*}
and
r\in\mathbf{R}
, let
r\cdot x_{n}^{*}\coloneqq(r\cdot x_{n})^{*}
.

Remark 6.2
.

This setup is not quite analogous to that of Section
4
: we have an action of
\mathbf{R}\times\langle\ast\rangle
on
Y_{n}
rather than an action of
\mathbf{R}\rtimes_{\alpha}\langle\ast\rangle
, where
\alpha:\mathbf{R}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
is the order reversing automorphism given by
\alpha(r)=-r
for all
r\in\mathbf{R}
.

Recall that, for each
n
,
\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}
denotes the set of equivalence classes of realisations of sequences
(x_{p})_{p\in P}\subseteq Y_{n}
, where
P\subseteq[0,\ell]
is a template and
\ell>0
(see Section
4.3
).

Definition 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\coloneqq\{\mathfrak{f}=(\mathfrak{f}_{n})_{n\in N}\in\prod_{n\in N}\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}:\sum_{n\in N}\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{n})<\infty\}
. For each
n\in N
, let
\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})\coloneqq\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{n})
. Let
\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{f})\coloneqq(\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f}))_{n\in N}\in\mathbf{R}
; we call
\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{f})
the
outline
of
\mathfrak{f}
.
Let
\id\in\mathcal{F}
be the unique element with outline 0.

Set
\mathfrak{f}\preceq\mathfrak{g}
if and only if
\mathfrak{f}_{n}\preceq\mathfrak{g}_{n}
for all
n\in N
.

Definition 6.4

(Signed length, signed outline)
.

Given
n\in N
and
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}
, let
P\subseteq[0,\ell(\mathfrak{f})]
be a template and
(x_{p})_{p\in P}
be a sequence with realisation
\mathfrak{f}
. For each
p\in P-\{\ell\}
, let
p^{\prime}\in P
be the successor of
p
. Define:

\displaystyle\ell^{+}(\mathfrak{f})\coloneqq\sum\{p^{\prime}-p:p\in P-\{\ell\}\text{ and }x_{p}\in X_{n}\}

\displaystyle\ell^{-}(\mathfrak{f})\coloneqq\sum\{p^{\prime}-p:p\in P-\{\ell\}\text{ and }x_{p}\in X_{n}^{*}\}

The
signed length
of
\mathfrak{f}
is
\sigma(\mathfrak{f})\coloneqq\ell^{+}(\mathfrak{f})-\ell^{-}(\mathfrak{f})\in\mathbb{R}
.

If
\mathfrak{f}=(\mathfrak{f}_{n})_{n\in N}\in\mathcal{F}
then the
signed outline
of
\mathfrak{f}
is
\tau(\mathfrak{f})\coloneqq(\sigma(\mathfrak{f}_{n}))_{n\in N}\in\mathbf{R}
.

Definition 6.5
.

•

Given
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{F}
, let
\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\coloneqq(\mathfrak{f}_{n}^{-1})_{n\in N}
, where
f_{n}^{-1}:[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})]\rightarrow X_{n}
is defined by
f_{n}^{-1}(t)=-\tau(\mathfrak{f})\cdot f_{n}^{*}(\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})-t)
for each
n
.

•

Define an operation
\square:\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
as follows. Let
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{F}
and, for each
n
, let
a_{n}:[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})+\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g})]\rightarrow X_{n}
be the map given by

a_{n}(t)=\begin{cases}f_{n}(t)\quad&\text{if }t\in[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})];\\
\tau(\mathfrak{f})\cdot g_{n}(t-\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f}))\quad&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

and let
\mathfrak{a}_{n}\in\mathcal{Y}_{X_{n}}
be the equivalence class of
a_{n}
. Define
\mathfrak{f}\square\mathfrak{g}\coloneqq(\mathfrak{a_{n}})_{n\in N}
.

Remark 6.6
.

If
\mathfrak{f}=(\mathfrak{f}_{n})_{n\in N},\mathfrak{g}=(\mathfrak{g}_{n})_{n\in N}\in\mathcal{F}
, then
\tau(\mathfrak{f}\square\mathfrak{g})=\tau(\mathfrak{f})+\tau(\tau(\mathfrak{f})\cdot\mathfrak{g})=\tau(\mathfrak{f})+\tau(\mathfrak{g})
, since
X_{n}
and
X_{n}^{*}
are
\mathbf{R}
-invariant for all
n\in N
.

Remark 6.7
.

If
\mathfrak{f}=(\mathfrak{f}_{n})_{n\in N}\in\mathcal{F}
then
\tau(\mathfrak{f}^{-1})=\tau(\mathfrak{f}^{*})=-\tau(\mathfrak{f})
.

Lemma 6.8
.

(\mathcal{F},\square)
is a cancellative monoid with identity
\id
and with involution
-1
.

Proof.

Given
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{F}
, we have
\mathfrak{o((f\square g)\square h)}=\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{f})+\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{g})+\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{h})=\mathfrak{o(f\square(g\square h))}
. Using Remark
6.6
, we have that, for each
n\in N
and
t\in[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})+\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g})+\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{h})]
:

\displaystyle((f\square g)\square h)_{n}(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}f_{n}(t)\quad&\text{if }t\in[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})];\\
\tau(\mathfrak{f})\cdot g_{n}(t-\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f}))&\text{if }t\in(\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f}),\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g})];\\
(\tau(\mathfrak{f})+\tau(\mathfrak{g}))\cdot h(t-\ell_{n}(f)-\ell_{n}(g))&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=(f\square(g\square h))_{n}(t).

Therefore
((\mathfrak{f}\square\mathfrak{g})\square\mathfrak{h})_{n}=(\mathfrak{f}\square(\mathfrak{g}\square\mathfrak{h}))_{n}
for each
n\in N
, which implies that
\mathfrak{(f\square g)\square h=f\square(g\square h)}
. Thus
\square
is associative. It is clear from the definitions that
\id
is a two-sided identity for
\square
, so
(\mathcal{F},\square)
is a monoid.

Let
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{F}
. Then
(\mathfrak{f}^{-1})^{-1}=(\mathfrak{a}_{n})_{n\in N}
has outline
\mathfrak{o}(\mathfrak{f})
. Using Remark
6.7
, we have that, for each
n\in N
and
t\in[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})]
,

a_{n}(t)=-\tau(\mathfrak{f}^{-1})\cdot(-\tau(\mathfrak{f})\cdot f_{n}^{*}(\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})-(\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})-t)))^{*}=\tau(\mathfrak{f})\cdot(-\tau(\mathfrak{f})\cdot f_{n}(t))=f_{n}(t).

Therefore
(\mathfrak{f}^{-1})^{-1}=\mathfrak{f}
and
-1:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
is an involution.
Fix
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{F}
. Then
(\mathfrak{f}\square\mathfrak{g})^{-1}=(\mathfrak{a}_{n})_{n\in N}
, where for each
i
,
a_{n}:[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})+\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g})]\rightarrow X_{n}
is defined by

\displaystyle a_{n}(t)

\displaystyle=-\tau(\mathfrak{f}\square\mathfrak{g})\cdot(f\square g)_{n}^{*}(\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})+\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g})-t)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-(\tau(\mathfrak{f})+\tau(\mathfrak{g}))\cdot(\tau(\mathfrak{f})\cdot g_{n}(\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g})-t))^{*}\quad&\text{if }t\in[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g}));\\
-(\tau(\mathfrak{f})+\tau(\mathfrak{g}))\cdot f_{n}^{*}(\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})-(t-\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g})))\quad&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}g_{n}^{-1}(t)\quad&\text{if }t\in[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g}));\\
\tau(\mathfrak{g}^{-1})\cdot f_{n}^{-1}(t-\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g}))\quad&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=(g^{-1}\square f^{-1})_{n},\quad\text{unless }t=\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g}).

Therefore
(\mathfrak{f}\square\mathfrak{g})^{-1}=\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\square\mathfrak{f}^{-1}
.

Finally, let
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{F}
and suppose that
\mathfrak{f}\square\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{f}\square\mathfrak{h}
. Then
\mathfrak{o(g)=o(h)}
and, for each
n
and for all but countably many
t\in[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g})]=[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{h})]
, we have that
\tau(f)\cdot g_{n}(t)=\tau(f)\cdot h_{n}(t)
so
g_{n}(t)=h_{n}(t)
. Therefore
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}
. It is clear that if
\mathfrak{f}\square\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}\square\mathfrak{g}
then
\mathfrak{f}=\mathfrak{h}
, so
(\mathcal{F},\preceq)
is cancellative.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

If
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{F}
is admissible and
r\in\mathbf{R}
, then the equivalence class
r\cdot\mathfrak{f}
of
(r\cdot f_{n})_{n\in N}
is admissible.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{F}
are such that
r\cdot\mathfrak{f}=\mathfrak{a}\square\mathfrak{b}\square\mathfrak{b}^{-1}\square\mathfrak{c}
. Since
X_{n}
and
X_{n}^{*}
are
\mathbf{R}
-invariant for each
n\in N
, we have
\tau(\mathfrak{g})=\tau(-r\cdot\mathfrak{g})
for all
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{F}
. It follows that
\mathfrak{f}=-r\cdot\mathfrak{a}\square-r\cdot\mathfrak{b}\square-r\cdot\mathfrak{b}^{-1}\square-r\cdot\mathfrak{c}
. But
(-r\cdot\mathfrak{b})^{-1}=(\mathfrak{a}_{n})_{n\in N}
where, for each
n\in N
and
t\in[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{b})]
,

\displaystyle a_{n}(t)

\displaystyle=-\tau(-r\cdot\mathfrak{b})\cdot(-r\cdot b_{n}(\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{b})-t))^{*}

\displaystyle=(-\tau(\mathfrak{b})-r)\cdot b_{n}^{*}(\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{b})-t)

\displaystyle=-r\cdot b_{n}^{-1}(t).

Thus
(-r\cdot\mathfrak{b})^{-1}=-r\cdot\mathfrak{b}
and, since
\mathfrak{f}
is admissible, this implies that
\mathfrak{b}=\id
.
∎

Lemma 6.10
.

(
\mathcal{F},\preceq,\id,\square,-1)
is an ore.

Proof.

(O2) holds by Lemma
6.8
. Let us go through the remaining axioms.

(O1)

This follows from the fact that
(\mathcal{Y}_{X_{i}},\preceq)
is a median semilattice for each
i
(by Lemma
4.5
).

(O3)

Let
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{F}
. It is clear from the definition of
\square
that, if there exists
\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\mathfrak{f}=\mathfrak{g}\square\mathfrak{h}
, then
\mathfrak{g}\preceq\mathfrak{f}
. Conversely, suppose that
\mathfrak{g}\preceq\mathfrak{f}
. For each
n\in N
define
h_{n}:[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})-\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g})]\rightarrow X
by
h(t)\coloneqq-\tau(\mathfrak{g})\cdot f(t+\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g}))
. Then
\mathfrak{f}=\mathfrak{g}\square\mathfrak{h}
.

(O4)

By Lemma
4.5
, each
(\mathcal{Y}_{X_{n}},\preceq)
is a median semilattice. Let
m_{n}:\mathcal{Y}_{X_{n}}^{3}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{X_{n}}
be the median map from Theorem
3.5
. For each
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{F}
let
m(\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})\coloneqq(m_{n}(\mathfrak{f}_{n},\mathfrak{g}_{n},\mathfrak{h}_{n}))_{n\in N}
. One can check that
m(\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})=(\mathfrak{f}\wedge\mathfrak{g})\vee(\mathfrak{g}\wedge\mathfrak{h})\vee(\mathfrak{f}\wedge\mathfrak{h})
. Let
\mathfrak{x=(x_{n})_{n\in N}}\in\mathcal{F}
. Then
m(\mathfrak{x\wedge f,x\wedge g,x\wedge h})=(m_{n}(\mathfrak{x}_{n}\wedge\mathfrak{f}_{n},\mathfrak{x}_{n}\wedge\mathfrak{g}_{n},\mathfrak{x}_{n}\wedge\mathfrak{h}_{n}))_{n\in N}=(\mathfrak{x}_{n}\wedge m_{n}(\mathfrak{f}_{n},\mathfrak{g}_{n},\mathfrak{h}_{n}))_{n\in N}=\mathfrak{x}\wedge m(\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h})
.

(O5)

Let
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{F}
and suppose that
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}
are admissible,
\mathfrak{f}\perp\mathfrak{h}
and
\mathfrak{f}\vee\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{f}\square\mathfrak{g}
. Let
I_{\mathfrak{f}}\coloneqq\{n\in N:\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})>0\}
and
I_{\mathfrak{h}}\coloneqq\{n\in N:\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{h})>0\}
. Then
I_{\mathfrak{f}}\cap I_{\mathfrak{h}}=\emptyset
and
\mathfrak{f}\vee\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{a}
where
\mathfrak{a}_{n}=\mathfrak{f}_{n}
if
n\in I_{\mathfrak{f}}
,
\mathfrak{a}_{n}=\mathfrak{h}_{n}
if
n\in I_{\mathfrak{h}}
and
a_{n}:\{0\}\rightarrow X_{n}
is any map of length 0 otherwise. Therefore, up to choosing a different representative for
\mathfrak{g}
, we have that
\mathfrak{h}=\tau(\mathfrak{f})\cdot\mathfrak{g}
. By Remark
6.9
,
\mathfrak{h}
is admissible.

(O6)

Let
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{F}
be admissible and orthogonal. Let
I_{\mathfrak{f}}\coloneqq\{n\in N:\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})>0\}
and
I_{\mathfrak{g}}\coloneq\{n\in N:\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{g})>0\}
. Then
I_{\mathfrak{f}}\cap I_{\mathfrak{g}}=\emptyset
and
\mathfrak{f}\vee\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}
where
\mathfrak{h}_{n}=\mathfrak{f}_{n}
if
n\in I_{f}
,
\mathfrak{h}_{n}=\mathfrak{g}_{n}
if
n\in I_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
h_{n}:\{0\}\rightarrow X_{n}
is any map of length 0 otherwise. It follows that
\mathfrak{f}\vee\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{f}\square-\tau(\mathfrak{f})\cdot\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}\square-\tau(\mathfrak{g})\cdot\mathfrak{f}
. By Remark
6.9
,
-\tau(\mathfrak{f})\cdot\mathfrak{g}
and
-\tau(\mathfrak{g})\cdot\mathfrak{f}
are admissible. Also,
\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\vee-\tau(f)\cdot\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\square\mathfrak{g}=-\tau(f)\cdot\mathfrak{g}\square-\tau(\mathfrak{g})\cdot\mathfrak{f}^{-1}
the fact that
I_{\mathfrak{f}}\cap I_{\mathfrak{g}}=\emptyset
implies that
\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\perp-\tau(\mathfrak{f})\cdot\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{g}^{-1}\perp-\tau(\mathfrak{g})\cdot\mathfrak{f}
.
∎

Definition 6.11
.

Let
(G,\star)
be the group extracted from
(\mathcal{F},\square)
.

Remark 6.12
.

Any element
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{F}
such that each
\mathfrak{f}_{n}
is constant is admissible and is therefore an element of
G
. Indeed if
\mathfrak{f}
is inadmissible then the image of some
\mathfrak{f}_{n}
must contain both some
x\in X_{n}
and an element in the orbit of
x^{*}
, which is contained in
X_{n}^{*}
, so
\mathfrak{f}_{n}
is not constant.

Let
\ell:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the map defined by
\ell(\mathfrak{f})=\sum_{n\in N}\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})
. Then
\ell
is a length function. Let
d
be the resulting metric on
G
given by
d(\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g})=\ell(\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\star\mathfrak{g})
for all
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}\in G
(see Proposition
3.29
).

We will need a few lemmas to prove the following:

Proposition 6.13
.

The metric space
(G,d)
is the
\ell^{1}
product of
|N|
complete real trees, with respect to some basepoint.

Definition 6.14
.

For each
n\in N
, let
\mathcal{Z}_{n}\coloneqq\{\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{F}:\mathfrak{f}_{i}=\id\;\forall\;i\neq n\}
and let
\psi_{n}:\mathcal{Z}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}
be the bijection given by
\psi_{n}(\mathfrak{f})=\mathfrak{f}_{n}
for all
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}
.

Let
T_{n}\coloneqq\{\mathfrak{f}_{n}\in\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}:\psi_{n}^{-1}(\mathfrak{f}_{n})\in G\}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}
for each
n\in N
.

Lemma 6.15
.

1.

There is an operation
\square
and an involution
-1
on
\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}
such that
(\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}},\preceq,

\square,-1,\id)
is an ore and
T_{n}
is its set of admissible elements.

2.

Let
(T_{n},\star)
be the group extracted from
\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}
. Then
\ell
is a length function on
T_{n}
and, if
d_{n}
is the metric on
T_{n}
given by
d_{n}(\mathfrak{f}_{n},\mathfrak{g}_{n})=\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f}_{n}^{-1}\star\mathfrak{g}_{n})
for all
\mathfrak{f}_{n},\mathfrak{g}_{n}\in T_{n}
then
(T_{n},d_{n})
is a complete real tree.

3.

If
|X_{n}|=1
then
T_{n}
is a line and if
2\leq|X_{n}|\leq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
then
T_{n}
is the universal real tree with valence
2^{\aleph_{0}}
.

Proof.

1.

Observe that
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
is invariant under
\square
and
-1
and that
\id\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}
. Also if
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mathfrak{g}\preceq\mathfrak{f}
then
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}
. Thus it follows from Lemma
6.10
that
(\mathcal{Z}_{n},\preceq,\square,-1,\id)
is an ore. The bijection
\psi_{n}
then endows
\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}
with the structure of an ore. By definition, the set of admissible elements of
\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}
is
T_{n}
.

2.

It is immediate from the definition that
\ell
is a length function on
\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}
. Thus, by Proposition
3.29
,
(T_{n},d_{n})
is a median space. For all
\mathfrak{f}_{n}\in T_{n}
we have
\mathfrak{f}_{n}^{\perp}=\{\id\}
so, by Lemma
3.31
,
T_{n}
has rank 1. Given
\mathfrak{f}_{n}\in T_{n}
and
t\in[0,\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{n})]
, let
\gamma(t)\coloneqq\mathfrak{g}_{n}
, where
g_{n}=f_{n}|_{[0,t]}
. Then
\mathfrak{g}_{n}\in T_{n}
and
\gamma:[0,\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{n})]\rightarrow T_{n}
is a geodesic from
\id
to
\mathfrak{f}_{n}
. Therefore
T_{n}
is an
\mathbb{R}
-tree by Lemma
2.11
.

The argument to show that
T_{n}
is complete is very similar to the argument showing that
T_{X}(Y)
is closed in the proof of Proposition
4.29
: Suppose that
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq T_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence and, for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, let
P_{i}\subseteq[0,\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{i})]
be a template and
(a_{p})_{p\in P_{i}}\subseteq Y_{n}
be a sequence with realisation
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
. Since
T_{n}
is downward closed, we can assume that
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is strictly increasing. Refine each
P_{i}
and
(a_{p})_{p\in P_{i}}
using Lemma
4.28
so that
\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{i-1}),\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{i})\in\overline{P_{i}}
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\mathfrak{a}_{0}\coloneq\id_{n}
. Then let
P\coloneqq\cup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}(P_{i}\cap[\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{i-1}),\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{i})])
, observe that
P
is a template for
[0,\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{i})]
, and let
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}
be the equivalence class of the sequence
(a_{p})_{p\in P}
. It follows from the admissibility of the
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
’s that
\mathfrak{a}\in T_{n}
. By construction,
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
\mathfrak{a}
.

3.

Suppose that
|X_{n}|=1
and let
L\coloneqq\{\mathfrak{f}_{n}\in\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}:\mathfrak{f}_{n}
is constant
\}
. By Remark
6.12
,
L\subseteq T_{n}
and the fact that
|X_{n}|=1
implies that
L
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}
. Let
\ell>0
, let
P\subseteq[0,\ell]
be a template and let
\mathfrak{f}_{n}\in\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}
be the realisation of a sequence
(y_{p})_{p\in P}\subseteq Y_{n}
. Suppose the CB-rank of
P
is as small as possible. If
\mathfrak{f}_{n}
is not constant then there exists
p_{1},p_{2}\in P-\{\ell\}
such that
p_{2}
is the successor of
p_{1}
and either
y_{p_{1}}\in X_{n}
and
y_{p_{2}}\in X_{n}^{*}
or
y_{p_{1}}\in X_{n}^{*}
and
y_{p_{2}}\in X_{n}
. In either case, this implies that
\mathfrak{f}
is inadmissible. Therefore
T_{n}=L
.

If
2\leq|X_{n}|\leq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
then it follows by the same argument as the proof of Proposition
4.31
that
T_{n}
is the universal real tree with valence
2^{\aleph_{0}}
.
∎

Lemma 6.16
.

Consider
\mathfrak{f}=(\mathfrak{f}_{n})_{n\in N}\in\mathcal{F}
. Then
\mathfrak{f}
is admissible if and only if
\mathfrak{f}_{n}\in T_{n}
for each
n\in N
.

Proof.

Suppose
\mathfrak{f}
is admissible and there exists
n\in N
such that
\mathfrak{f}_{n}=\mathfrak{a}_{n}\square\mathfrak{b}_{n}\square\mathfrak{b}_{n}^{-1}\square\mathfrak{c}_{n}
for some
\mathfrak{a}_{n},\mathfrak{b}_{n},\mathfrak{c}_{n}\in\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}
. For each
i\in N-\{n\}
define
\mathfrak{a}_{i}\coloneqq\mathfrak{b}_{i}\coloneqq\id
and
\mathfrak{c}_{i}\coloneqq(-\sigma(\mathfrak{a}_{n})\chi_{n})\cdot\mathfrak{f}_{i}
. Let
\mathfrak{a}\coloneqq(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i\in N},\mathfrak{b}\coloneqq(\mathfrak{b}_{i})_{i\in N}
and
\mathfrak{c}\coloneqq(\mathfrak{c}_{i})_{i\in N}
. Then
\mathfrak{f}=\mathfrak{a}\square\mathfrak{b}\square\mathfrak{b}^{-1}\square\mathfrak{c}
, so
\mathfrak{b}_{n}=\id
.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathfrak{f}_{n}\in T_{n}
for all
n\in N
. Let
\mathfrak{a},\mathfrak{b},\mathfrak{c}\in\mathcal{F}
be such that
\mathfrak{f}=\mathfrak{a}\square\mathfrak{b}\square\mathfrak{b}^{-1}\square\mathfrak{c}
. For each
n\in N
,
\mathfrak{f}_{n}
is the equivalence class of the map
f_{n}:[0,\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f})]\rightarrow Y_{n}
given by:

\displaystyle f_{n}(t)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}a_{n}(t)\quad&\text{if }t\in[0,\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n})];\\
\tau(\mathfrak{a})\cdot b_{n}(t-\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n}))&\text{if }t\in(\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n}),\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n})+\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n})];\\
(\tau(\mathfrak{a})+\tau(\mathfrak{b}))\cdot b_{n}^{-1}(t-\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n})-\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n}))&\text{if }t\in(\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n})+\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n}),\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n})+2\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n})];\\
\tau(\mathfrak{a})\cdot c_{n}(t-\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n})-2\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n}))&\text{if }t\in(\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n})+2\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n}),\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{n})]\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}a_{n}(t)\quad&\text{if }t\in[0,\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n})];\\
\tau(\mathfrak{a})\cdot b_{n}(t-\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n}))&\text{if }t\in(\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n}),\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n})+\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n})];\\
\tau(\mathfrak{a})\cdot b_{n}^{*}(2\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n})-t+\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n}))&\text{if }t\in(\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n})+\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n}),\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n})+2\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n})];\\
\tau(\mathfrak{a})\cdot c_{n}(t-\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n})-2\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n}))&\text{if }t\in(\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{n})+2\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n}),\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{n})]\end{cases}

for all
t\in[0,\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{n})]
. Let
\widehat{\tau}\in\mathbf{R}
be such that
\widehat{\tau}(n)=\tau(\mathfrak{a})(n)
and
\widehat{\tau}(i)=0
if
i\neq n
. Let
\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}_{n}\coloneqq\widehat{\tau}\cdot\mathfrak{b}_{n}\in\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{c}}_{n}\coloneqq\widehat{\tau}\cdot\mathfrak{c}_{n}\in\mathcal{Z}_{Y_{n}}
. Then
{\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}_{n}}^{-1}
is the equivalence class of the map
[0,\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n})]\rightarrow Y_{n}
given by
t\mapsto(\widehat{\tau}-\tau(\psi_{n}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}_{n})))\cdot b_{n}^{*}(\ell(\mathfrak{b}_{n})-t)
. It follows that

\mathfrak{a}_{n}\square\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}_{n}\square(\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}_{n})^{-1}\square\widehat{\mathfrak{c}}_{n}=\mathfrak{f}_{n}
. Therefore
\widehat{\mathfrak{b}}_{n}=\id
which, by Remark
6.9
, implies that
\mathfrak{b}_{n}=\id
. As this holds for all
n\in N
, we have that
\mathfrak{b}=\id
and
\mathfrak{f}
is admissible.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.13
.

Let
\mathcal{T}\coloneqq(T_{n})_{n\in N}
.
Lemma
6.16
implies that, as a set,
G
is the
\ell^{1}
product
\ell^{1}(\mathcal{T},\id)
.
For all
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}\in G
we have
d(\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g})=\ell(\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\star\mathfrak{g})=\sum_{n\in N}d_{n}(\mathfrak{f}_{n},\mathfrak{g}_{n})
. Therefore
d
is precisely the
\ell^{1}
metric on
G=\ell^{1}(\mathcal{T},\id)
and, by Lemma
6.15
, each
T_{n}
is a complete real tree.
∎

Definition 6.17
.

A
standard flat
in
G
is a maximal flat
F\subseteq G
of the form

F=\{\mathfrak{f}\in G:\mathfrak{f}_{n}\text{ is constant with image }x_{n}\text{ or }x_{n}^{*}\;\forall\;n\in N\},

for some
(x_{n})_{n\in N}\in\prod_{n\in N}X_{n}
. We denote
F((x_{n})_{n\in N})\coloneqq F
.

The stabilisers of maximal flats are straightforward to describe:

Lemma 6.18
.

Let
(x_{n})_{n\in N}\in\prod_{n\in N}X_{n}
and let
F\coloneqq F((x_{n})_{n\in N})
. Then

\Stab_{G}(F)=\{\mathfrak{f}\in F:\tau(\mathfrak{f})\in\cap_{n\in N}\Stab_{\mathbf{R}}(x_{n})\}\cong\cap_{n\in N}\Stab_{\mathbf{R}}(x_{n}).

The restriction of
\tau
to
F
is an isometry
F\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
and the restriction
\tau|_{\Stab_{G}(F)}:\Stab_{G}(F))\rightarrow\cap_{n\in N}\Stab_{\mathbf{R}}(x_{n})
is an isomorphism.

Lemma 6.19
.

Let
F\subseteq G
be a maximal flat and let
H\leq\mathbf{R}
be such that
\Stab_{G}(F)\curvearrowright F
is isomorphic to
H\curvearrowright\mathbf{R}
. If
H
is dense in
\mathbf{R}
then
F
is a translate of a standard flat.

Proof.

We can replace
F
by a
G
-translate so that
\id\in F
. Note that
\Stab_{G}(F)\subseteq F
in this case. Let
\mathfrak{f}\in\Stab_{G}(F)
be non-trivial. Up to translating
F
by another element of
G
, we can assume that, for all
n\in N
, there exists a non-trivial element
\mathfrak{f}_{n}^{\prime}\preceq\mathfrak{f}_{n}
which is constant, say with image
y_{n}\in Y_{n}
.

Let
\varphi:F\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
be an isometry such that
\varphi(\id)=0
and, for each
n\in N
, we have
\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{R}\cdot\chi_{n})=\cap_{i\neq n}p_{i}^{-1}(\id)
.

Let
E\coloneqq\varphi(\Stab_{G}(F))
and note that
E\in[H]
, so in particular
E\in\Sub_{D}(\mathbf{R})
.
For each
n\in N
let
p_{n}:\mathbf{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the projection defined by
p_{n}((t_{i})_{i\in N})=t_{n}
. The image
p_{n}(E)
is dense in
\mathbb{R}
for each
n\in N
.
Fix
n\in N
and let
\mathfrak{g}\in\Stab_{G}(F)
be such that
\id\precneq\mathfrak{g}_{n}\precneq\mathfrak{f}_{n}^{\prime}
. Let
\mathfrak{h}\coloneqq\mathfrak{g}\star\mathfrak{f}
, so for all but countably many
t\in[0,\ell(\mathfrak{g}_{n})]
,
h_{n}(t)=g_{n}(t)=y_{n}
and for all but countably many
t\in(\ell(\mathfrak{g}_{n}),\ell(\mathfrak{g}_{n})+\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{n})]
,
h_{n}(t)=\tau(g)\cdot y_{n}
. But
\mathfrak{h}\in F
,
\mathfrak{g}_{n}\preceq\mathfrak{h}
and
\ell(\mathfrak{h}_{n})>\ell_{n}(\mathfrak{f}_{n})
so
\mathfrak{f}_{n}\preceq\mathfrak{h}_{n}
, which implies that, for all but countably many
t\in[\ell(\mathfrak{g}_{n}),\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{n})]
, we have
h_{n}(t)=f_{n}(t)=y_{n}
. Therefore
\tau(\mathfrak{g})\cdot y_{n}=y_{n}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{n}
is constant with image
y_{n}
. It follows by induction on
k\in\mathbb{N}
that the
n
th coordinate of
\mathfrak{g}^{k}\star\mathfrak{f}
is constant with image
y_{n}
. It follows that the
n
th coordinate of
\mathfrak{g}^{k}
is constant with image
y_{n}
for all
k
and therefore the
n
th coordinate of
\mathfrak{g}^{-k}
is constant with image
y_{n}^{*}
. Thus, for all
\mathfrak{h}\in F
, the element
\mathfrak{h}_{n}
is constant with image
y_{n}
or
y_{n}^{*}
. The same argument holds for all
n\in N
so we have shown that
F=F((x_{n})_{n\in N})
, where, for each
n\in N
,
x_{n}\in X_{n}
is such that
y_{n}=x_{n}
or
y_{n}=x_{n}^{*}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Let
\iota:\overline{\Sub}_{D}(\mathbf{R})\rightarrow\mathcal{K},\eta:N\rightarrow\{0,1\}
be arbitrary maps such that
\eta
is non-zero. Fix
m\in N
such that
\eta(m)=1
.
For each
[H]\in\overline{\Sub}_{D}(\mathbf{R})
, fix a representative
H\leq\mathbf{R}
and let
B_{H}\coloneqq\mathbf{R}/H
, equipped with the natural action of
\mathbf{R}
by addition. Let
D_{H}
be the disjoint union of
\iota([H])
copies of
B_{H}
.
If
\iota
is the zero map then let
X_{m}\coloneqq B_{\{0\}}
, and if
\iota=\chi_{\mathbf{R}}
then let
X_{m}\coloneqq B_{\mathbf{R}}\sqcup B_{\{0\}}
. Otherwise, let
X_{m}\coloneqq\sqcup_{H\in\Sub_{D}(\mathbf{R})}D_{H}
.
If
n\in N-\{m\}
and
\eta(n)=1
, then let
X_{n}\coloneqq B_{\mathbf{R}}\sqcup B_{\{0\}}
. If
n\in N
and
\eta(n)=0
, then let
X_{n}\coloneqq B_{\mathbf{R}}
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the resulting ore and let
G
be its extracted group, equipped with the metric
d
defined above. Then
(G,d)
is the
\ell^{1}
product
\ell^{1}(\mathcal{T},\id)
. By Lemma
6.15
, for each
n\in N
, the metric space
(T_{n},d_{n})
is the complete universal real tree with valence
2^{\aleph_{0}}
if
\eta(n)=1
and
T_{n}
is isometric to
\mathbb{R}
if
\eta(n)=0
.

Fix a subgroup
H\in\Sub_{D}(\mathbf{R})
. Given
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\prod X_{n}
, it follows from Lemma
6.18
that, if
F=F((x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is the corresponding standard flat, then
G\cdot F\in A_{H}
if and only if
\Stab_{\mathbf{R}}(x_{m})\in[H]
and
x_{n}\in B_{\mathbf{R}}\subseteq X_{n}
for all
n\neq m
. Two standard flats
F((x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}),F((y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}})
whose stabilisers act with dense orbits
are in the same
G
-orbit if and only if
x_{m},y_{m}
are in the same
\mathbf{R}
-orbit. Therefore there are precisely
\iota([H])
orbits of standard flats in
A_{H}
. It follows from Lemma
6.19
that
|A_{H}|=\iota([H])
.
∎

6.2
Reducible actions

A natural question to ask when studying a group acting on a product space is whether it is “reducible”, either in the sense that it splits non-trivially as a direct product or that a subgroup large enough to encompass some of the geometry of the group splits non-trivially as a direct product. Given a group
G
acting properly cocompactly on the product of two locally finite simplicial trees, one says that
G
is
reducible
if a finite index subgroup of
G
splits non-trivially as a direct product. Inspired by this, we consider the following notions.
Fix a group
G
and a space
X
such that
X
is isometric to the
\ell^{1}
-product of two unbounded real trees
T_{1},T_{2}
.

An action of a group
G
on a finite rank median space
X
is called
essential
(or sometimes
minimal
, see
[
Fio24
]
) if there is no proper
G
-invariant convex subspace of
X
.

Definition 6.20
.

A free cobounded / essential action of
G
on
X
is
coboundedly / essentially reducible
if there exists a subgroup
H\leq G
which splits non-trivially as a direct product and such that the induced action of
H
on
X
is cobounded / essential.

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
T_{1},T_{2}
be
\mathbb{R}
-trees which are not isometric to
\mathbb{R}
and let
X\coloneqq T_{1}\times T_{2}
be their
\ell^{1}
product.
Let
H=H_{1}\times H_{2}\leq\Isom(T_{1})\times\Isom(T_{2})
be a subgroup such that
H_{1},H_{2}\neq\{\id\}
and the action of
H
on
X
is free and essential. For each
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
p_{i}:H\rightarrow\Isom(T_{i})
be the canonical projection. Then, up to relabelling
H_{1},H_{2}
, the following holds. Given any point
(z_{1},z_{2})\in X
, we have
H_{1}=\Stab_{H}(T_{1}\times\{z_{2}\}),\;H_{2}=\Stab_{H}(\{z_{1}\}\times T_{2})
and the induced actions
H_{1}\curvearrowright T_{1}\times\{z_{2}\},\;H_{2}\curvearrowright\{z_{1}\}\times T_{2}
are free and essential.

It follows that, if the action of
H
on
X
is in addition cobounded / cocompact / transitive, then the
H_{1}\curvearrowright T_{1}\times\{z_{2}\}
and the
H_{2}\curvearrowright\{z_{1}\}\times T_{2}
are cobounded / cocompact / transitive for each
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Proof.

Since the action of
H
on
T_{1}\times T_{2}
is essential, so are the actions
p_{1},p_{2}
. Let us show that there is a hyperbolic element in
p_{i}(H_{1}\cup H_{2})
for
i=1,2
. Suppose to the contrary that every element in
p_{i}(H_{1}\cup H_{2})
fixes a point in
T_{i}
. Let
h_{1}\in H_{1},h_{2}\in H_{2}
and
x_{1}\in T_{i}
be a point fixed by
p_{i}(h_{1})
. Then
p_{i}(h_{1})
fixes
p_{i}(h_{2})x_{1}
and therefore fixes the segment
[x_{1},p_{i}(h_{2})x_{1}]
pointwise. Since
p_{i}(h_{2})
is elliptic, the midpoint
x_{2}
of the (possibly degenerate) segment
[x_{1},p_{i}(h_{2})x_{1}]
is fixed by
p_{i}(h_{2})
as well as
p_{i}(h_{1})
. Thus
p_{i}(h_{1}h_{2})
fixes a point. But by
[
Fio24
, Theorem C(1)]
there is a hyperbolic element in
p_{i}(H)
so this is a contradiction.

Now, up to relabelling
H_{1},H_{2}
, we can assume that there exists an element
h\in H_{1}
such that
p_{1}(h)
is hyperbolic. Let
\ell\subseteq T_{1}
be the axis of
p_{1}(h)
. Then, since
H_{2}
commutes with
h
,
p_{1}(H_{2})
stabilises
\ell
. Moreover, if
p_{1}(H_{2})
contains a hyperbolic element
p_{1}(h_{2})
, then its axis is also
\ell
and, since
H_{1}
commutes with
H_{2}
, this implies that
p_{1}(H_{1})
also stabilises
\ell
. Since the action of
p_{1}(H)
is essential, this implies that
T_{1}=\ell
. So we can assume that
p_{1}(H_{2})
contains no hyperbolic elements. Since
p_{1}(H_{2})
stabilises
\ell
, it follows that there is a point
x_{0}\in\ell
which is fixed by every element of
p_{1}(H_{2})
. Let
h_{1}\in H_{1},h_{2},h_{2}^{\prime}\in H_{2}
. Then
p_{1}(h_{2}^{\prime})p_{1}(h_{1}h_{2})x_{0}=p_{1}(h_{1})x_{0}=p_{1}(h_{1}h_{2})x_{0}
. Therefore
p_{1}(H_{2})
fixes the
p_{1}(H)
-orbit of
x_{0}
pointwise and therefore its convex hull, which is the entirety of
T
. Note that this also implies that the action of
p_{1}(H_{1})
on
T_{1}
is essential.

Since the action of
H
on
T
is free, the action of
p_{2}(H_{2})
on
T_{2}
must be non-trivial. If
p_{2}(H_{1})
contains a hyperbolic element then the above argument implies that
T_{2}
is a line. Therefore
p_{2}(H_{1})
does not contain a hyperbolic element, so
p_{2}(H_{2})
does and the above argument implies that the action of
p_{2}(H_{2})
on
T_{2}
is essential while the action of
p_{2}(H_{1})
is trivial. The proposition follows.
∎

6.3
A free transitive and irreducible action

We can now prove the following:

Corollary 6.22
.

Let
T_{1}=T_{2}
be the complete universal real tree with valence
2^{\aleph_{0}}
. There exists a group
G\leq\Isom(T_{1})\times\Isom(T_{2})
which is essentially reducible but coboundedly irreducible.

Proof.

Let
X_{1}=X_{2}=\mathbb{R}
. Define an action of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
on
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
as follows. If
(r_{1},r_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
x_{1}\in X_{1}
and
x_{2}\in X_{2}
, then
(r_{1},r_{2})\cdot x_{1}=r_{2}+x_{1}
and
(r_{1},r_{2})\cdot x_{2}=r_{1}+x_{2}
. Let
(\mathcal{F},\square,-1,\preceq,\id)
be the ore constructed in Section
6.1
using these actions and let
(G,\star)
be the group extracted from
\mathcal{F}
. Let
d
be the metric on
G
, so that
(G,d)
is the
\ell^{1}
product
T_{1}\times T_{2}
.

For each
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
p_{i}:\Isom(T_{1})\times\Isom(T_{2})\rightarrow\Isom(T_{i})
be the canonical projection.
Suppose
H\leq G
splits non-trivially as a direct product and the induced action
H\curvearrowright T_{1}\times T_{2}
is essential. By Lemma
6.21
,
H=H_{1}\times H_{2}
, where
H_{1}=\ker(p_{2})\cap H
and
H_{2}=\ker(p_{1})\cap H
. Fix
i\in\{1,2\}
and let
\mathfrak{f}\in T_{i}
be a constant element with image
x_{i}\in X_{i}
and length
\ell>0
. If
\mathfrak{h}\in H_{i}
, then
\sigma(\mathfrak{h})=0
, so
\ell^{-}(\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\star\mathfrak{h})\geq\ell^{+}(\mathfrak{f})=\ell
. Therefore
d_{i}(\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{h})=\ell(\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\star\mathfrak{h})\geq\ell
. Thus the action of
H_{i}
on
T_{i}
is not cobounded, which implies that the action of
H
on
T_{1}\times T_{2}
is not cobounded.

To see that
G
is essentially reducible in spite of being coboundedly irreducible, let
H_{1}\coloneqq p_{1}(\ker(p_{2}))
and
H_{2}\coloneqq p_{2}(\ker(p_{1}))
. Fix
i\in\{1,2\}
. Let
\mathfrak{f}\in T_{1}
and let
(y_{p})_{p\in P}\subseteq Y_{i}
be the sequence whose realisation is
\mathfrak{f}
. For each
p\in P
, fix
y_{p}^{\prime}\in Y_{i}-\{y_{p}\}
such that, if
y_{p}\in X_{i}
then
y_{p}^{\prime}\in X_{i}
, and if
y_{p}\in X_{i}^{*}
then
y_{p}^{\prime}\in X_{i}^{*}
. Let
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
be the realisation of
(y_{p}^{\prime})_{p\in P}
. Then
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
is admissible,
\sigma({\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}}^{-1})=-\sigma(\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})=-\sigma(\mathfrak{f})
and
\mathfrak{f}^{-1}\wedge{\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}}^{-1}=\id
. Therefore
\mathfrak{f}\preceq\mathfrak{f}\square{\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}}^{-1}=\mathfrak{f}\star{\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}}^{-1}
and
\sigma(\mathfrak{f}\star{\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}}^{-1})=0
. It follows that
\mathfrak{f}\star{\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}}^{-1}\in H_{i}
. Therefore the action
H_{i}\curvearrowright T_{i}
is essential, which implies that
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
are non-trivial and the action of
H_{1}\times H_{2}\cong\langle H_{1}\times\{\id\},\{\id\}\times H_{2}\rangle\leq G
on
T_{1}\times T_{2}
is essential.
∎

6.4
Embedding BMW groups into products of
\mathbb{R}
-trees

The groups constructed in Section
6.1
contain no isometrically embedded irreducible BMW groups:

Proposition 6.23
.

Let
(G,\star)
be the group from Definition
6.11
. Let
H
be a BMW group with BMW presentation
\langle A\cup X|R\rangle
and suppose there exists a map
\psi:H\hookrightarrow G
which is both a homomorphism and an isometric embedding, where
H
is equipped with the word metric corresponding to
A\cup X
. Then
H
is reducible.

Proof.

Let
d_{H}
denote the word metric on
H
with respect to
A\cup X
.

We first show that we can assume without loss of generality that
N=\{1,2\}
,
G
is isometric to the
\ell^{1}
product
T_{1}\times T_{2}
and
\psi(\langle A\rangle)\subseteq T_{1}\times\{\id\}
and
\psi(\langle X\rangle)\subseteq\{\id\}\times T_{2}
.
The fact that
\psi
is an isometric embedding implies that, for all
a\in A\cup A^{-1}
and
x\in X\cup X^{-1}
we have
\ell(\psi(a))=\ell(\psi(x))=1
and
\ell(\psi(ax))=2
. Then

2=\ell(\psi(a^{-1}x))=\ell(\psi(a))+\ell(\psi(x))-2\ell(\psi(a)\wedge\psi(x))=2-2\ell(\psi(a)\wedge\psi(x)),

so
\psi(a)\wedge\psi(x)=\id
. Therefore
\psi(a),\psi(x)
concatenate geodesically for all
a\in A\cup A^{-1},x\in X\cup X^{-1}
. Moreover, if
a\in A\cup A^{-1},x\in X\cup X^{-1}
and
a^{\prime}\in A\cup A^{-1},x^{\prime}\in X\cup X^{-1}
are the unique elements such that
ax^{\prime}=xa^{\prime}
, then
\psi(a)\square\psi(x^{\prime})=\psi(a)\star\psi(x^{\prime})=\psi(x)\star\psi(a^{\prime})=\psi(x)\square\psi(a^{\prime})
. Therefore
\psi(a)\perp\psi(x)
. Both
\langle A\rangle
and
\langle X\rangle
are
\mathbb{Z}
-trees when equipped with
d_{H}
, so
\psi(\langle A\rangle)
and
\psi(\langle X\rangle)
are
\mathbb{Z}
-trees. Therefore there exists
n,m\in N
with
n\neq m
such that
\psi(\langle A\rangle)\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{n}
and
\psi(\langle X\rangle)\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{m}
. The set
\mathcal{E}
of elements
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\mathfrak{f}_{i}=\id
for all
i\in N
such that
i\notin\{n,m\}
is invariant under
\square
and
-1
, contains
\id
and, if
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{F}
such that
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{E}
and
\mathfrak{g}\preceq\mathfrak{f}
, then
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathcal{E}
. Thus
(E,\square,-1,\id,\preceq)
is an ore and its extracted group contains
\psi(H)
.

Thus, we assume from now on that
N=\{1,2\}
,
G
is isometric to the
\ell^{1}
product
T_{1}\times T_{2}
and
\psi(\langle A\rangle)\subseteq T_{1}\times\{\id\}
and
\psi(\langle X\rangle)\subseteq\{\id\}\times T_{2}
.

For all
g\in\langle A\rangle
,
f\in\langle X\rangle
, let
\mathfrak{g}_{1}\in T_{1},\mathfrak{f}_{2}\in T_{2}
be such that
\psi(g)=(\mathfrak{g}_{1},\id),\psi(f)=(\id,\mathfrak{f}_{2})
. Define

\displaystyle V_{A}\coloneqq\cup\{y\in Y_{1}:a_{1}(t)=y\text{ for some }a\in A\cup A^{-1}\text{ and uncountably many }t\in[0,\ell(\mathfrak{a}_{1})]\}

\displaystyle V_{X}\coloneqq\cup\{y\in Y_{2}:x_{2}(t)=y\text{ for some }x\in X\cup X^{-1}\text{ and uncountably many }t\in[0,\ell(\mathfrak{x}_{2})]\}.

Let
n\coloneqq\max\{(2|A|)!,(2|X|)!\}
.
If
g\in\langle A\rangle
and
x\in X\cup X^{-1}
, then there exists
f\in\langle A\rangle
such that
g^{n}x=xf
. Therefore

(\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{n},\tau(\psi(g^{n}))\cdot\mathfrak{x}_{2})=(\tau(\psi(x))\cdot\mathfrak{f}_{1},\mathfrak{x}_{2})

Thus
\tau(\psi(g^{n}))\cdot\mathfrak{x}_{2}=\mathfrak{x}_{2}
for all
g\in\langle A\rangle
and
x\in X\cup X^{-1}
.
By a symmetric argument,
\tau(\psi(g^{n}))\cdot\mathfrak{a}_{1}=\mathfrak{a}_{1}
for all
g\in\langle X\rangle
and
a\in A\cup A^{-1}
.

Consider the actions
\alpha_{A}:\langle A\rangle\rightarrow\Sym(Y_{2})
and
\alpha_{X}:\langle X\rangle\rightarrow\Sym(Y_{1})
defined by
\alpha_{A}(g)(y_{2})=\tau(\psi(g))\cdot y_{2}
and
\alpha_{X}(f)(y_{1})=\tau(\psi(f))\cdot y_{1}
for all
g\in\langle A\rangle,f\in\langle X\rangle,y_{1}\in Y_{1}
and
y_{2}\in Y_{2}
. Let
W_{A}\subseteq Y_{1}
be the
\langle X\rangle
-orbit of
V_{A}
and let
W_{X}
be the
\langle A\rangle
-orbit of
V_{X}
. Let
\beta_{A}
be the restriction of
\alpha_{A}
to
W_{X}
and let
\beta_{X}
be the restriction of
\alpha_{A}
to
W_{X}
.
Then, by the above argument, every
\langle A\rangle
-orbit in
W_{X}
has cardinality dividing
n
and every
\langle X\rangle
-orbit in
W_{A}
has cardinality dividing
n
. Since
A
and
X
are finite, there are finitely many subgroups of
\langle A\rangle
with index at most
n
and finitely many subgroups of
\langle X\rangle
with index at most
n
. Let
H_{1}\leq\langle A\rangle
be the intersection of all subgroups of
\langle A\rangle
with index at most
n
and let
H_{2}\leq\langle X\rangle
be the intersection of all subgroups of
\langle X\rangle
with index at most
n
. Then
H_{1}\leq\ker(\beta_{A})
and
H_{2}\leq\ker(\beta_{X})
and
H_{1}
has finite index in
\langle A\rangle
and
H_{2}
has finite index in
\langle X\rangle
.

Let
H^{\prime}\coloneqq\langle H_{1},H_{2}\rangle\leq H
. Then
H^{\prime}
is a finite index subgroup of
H
. Let
h\in H_{1}
and
f\in\langle X\rangle
. Let us show by induction on the word length of
f
that
\beta_{A}(h)\circ f_{2}\simeq f_{2}
. If
f\in X\cup X^{-1}
then this follows from the fact that
h\in\ker(\beta_{2})
, since
f_{2}(t)\in W_{X}
for all but countably many
t\in[0,\ell(\mathfrak{f}_{2})]
. Let
m>1
and suppose the word length of
f
is
m
. Then
f=f^{\prime}x
where
f^{\prime}\in\langle X\rangle
has word length
m-1
and
x\in X\cup X^{-1}
. By the induction hypothesis,
\psi(hf^{\prime})=(\mathfrak{h}_{1},\tau(\psi(h))\cdot\mathfrak{f}_{2}^{\prime})=(\mathfrak{h}_{1},\mathfrak{f}_{2}^{\prime})=\psi(f^{\prime})\star(-\tau(\psi(f^{\prime}))\cdot\mathfrak{h}_{1},\id)
. Moreover, for all
w\in W_{X}
, we have

\tau((-\tau(\psi(f^{\prime}))\cdot\mathfrak{h}_{1},\id))\cdot w=\tau(\psi({f^{\prime}}^{-1}hf^{\prime}))\cdot w=w.

Therefore:

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{h}_{1},\tau(\mathfrak{h}_{1})\cdot\mathfrak{f}_{2})

\displaystyle=\psi(hf)

\displaystyle=\psi(hf^{\prime}x)

\displaystyle=\psi(f^{\prime})\star(-\tau(\psi(f^{\prime}))\cdot\mathfrak{h}_{1},\tau((-\tau(\psi(f^{\prime}))\cdot\mathfrak{h}_{1},\id))\cdot\mathfrak{x}_{2})

\displaystyle=\psi(f^{\prime})\star(-\tau(\psi(f^{\prime}))\cdot\mathfrak{h}_{1},\mathfrak{x}_{2})

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{h}_{1},\mathfrak{f}_{2}),

so
\beta_{A}(h)\circ f_{2}\simeq f_{2}
. A symmetric argument shows that
\beta_{X}(h)\circ g_{1}\simeq g_{1}
for all
h\in H_{2}
and
g\in\langle A\rangle
. It follows that
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
commute, so
H\cong H_{1}\times H_{2}
.
∎

I do not know whether it is possible to isometrically embed an irreducible BMW group into a group acting freely and transitively on a product of two (complete) real trees (see Question
1.2
). If one requests only that
G
acts on the product with dense orbits, the following theorem shows that this can be done for BMW groups equipped with a positive BMW presentation. Note that the group defined by Wise in
[
Wis07
, Example 4.1]
has a positive BMW presentation and is irreducible by Corollary 6.8 in loc. sit.

Theorem 6.24
.

Let
H
be a BMW group with a positive BMW presentation
\langle A\cup X\;|\;R\rangle
and let
\Cay(H,A\cup X)
be the corresponding Cayley graph.
There exists a group
G
such that the following hold:

i.

there is an injective homomorphism
H\hookrightarrow G
;

ii.

G
acts freely with dense orbits on the
\ell^{1}
product of two
\mathbb{R}
-trees
T_{1}\times T_{2}
;

iii.

there is an isometric embedding
\psi:\Cay(H,A\cup X)\hookrightarrow T_{1}\times T_{2}
, which is equivariant relative to
H\hookrightarrow G
.

If
H
is irreducible, then for any subgroup
L\leq G
which splits non-trivially as a direct product, the induced action of
L
on
T_{1}\times T_{2}
does not have dense orbits.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be closure of
R
under cyclic permutations.
Let
\Gamma
be the Cayley complex of
H
with respect to the presentation
\langle A\sqcup X\;|\;R\rangle
and recall that
\Gamma=\Gamma_{A}\times\Gamma_{X}
is the product of the Cayley graph
\Gamma_{A}
of
\langle A\rangle
with the Cayley graph
\Gamma_{X}
of
\langle X\rangle
. Let
\pi_{A}:\Gamma\rightarrow\Gamma_{A}
and
\pi_{X}:\Gamma\rightarrow\Gamma_{X}
be the projection maps and let
\id_{A},\id_{X}
be the identity in
\langle A\rangle,\langle X\rangle
respectively. The action of
H
on
\Gamma
does not permute the factors so there are projection actions
H\curvearrowright\Gamma_{A}
and
H\curvearrowright\Gamma_{X}
defined (on the vertex sets) by

h\cdot g_{A}=\pi_{A}(h(g_{A},\id_{X})),\quad h\cdot g_{X}=\pi_{X}(h(\id_{A},g_{X}))\quad\forall\;h\in H,g_{A}\in\langle A\rangle,g_{X}\in\langle X\rangle.

Definition 6.25
.

The induced action of
\langle A\rangle
on
\Gamma_{X}
permutes the set
X
and can be read off the presentation of
H
: for all
a\in A
and
x\in X
, there exist a unique
a^{\prime}\in A
and a unique
x^{\prime}\in X
such that
ax{a^{\prime}}^{-1}{x^{\prime}}^{-1}\in\mathcal{R}
, and we then have
a\cdot x=x^{\prime}
. Similarly the action of
\langle X\rangle
on
\Gamma_{A}
permutes the set
A
and can be read off the presentation: given
a\in A,x\in X
there exist unique
a^{\prime}\in A,x^{\prime}\in X
such that
a^{\prime}x^{\prime}a^{-1}x^{-1}\in\mathcal{R}
and
x\cdot a=a^{\prime}
.

For each
a\in A
and
x\in X
let
\sigma_{a}\in\Sym(X)
and
\sigma_{x}\in\Sym(A)
denote the resulting permutations.

Definition 6.26
.

•

Let
Y_{A}
be a graph with vertex set
A
such that, for each
a_{1},a_{2}\in A
with
a_{1}\neq a_{2}
, there is a directed edge
e(a_{1},a_{2})
from
a_{1}
to
a_{2}
of length
1
. Similarly, let
Y_{X}
be a graph with vertex set
X
such that, for each
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
with
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
, there is a directed edge
e(x_{1},x_{2})
from
x_{1}
to
x_{2}
of length
1
.
Let
d
denote the resulting metrics on
Y_{A}
and
Y_{X}
.

•

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
Y_{A}(n)\subseteq Y_{A},Y_{X}(n)\subseteq Y_{X}
be the subsets consisting of points whose distance to a vertex is a multiple of
1/n
.
For all distinct
a_{1},a_{2}\in A
,
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
let
e_{n}(a_{1},a_{2})\coloneqq Y_{A}(n)\cap e(a_{1},a_{2})
and
e_{n}(x_{1},x_{2})\coloneqq Y_{X}(n)\cap e(x_{1},x_{2})
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We will construct a BMW group
G_{n}
with generating set
Y_{A}(n)\sqcup Y_{X}(n)
.
We first define actions of the free groups
\langle Y_{A}(n)\rangle
and
\langle Y_{X}(n)\rangle
on
Y_{X}(n)
and
Y_{A}(n)
respectively.

Recall that a
directed cycle
in a directed graph is a cycle with edges
(e_{0},\dots,e_{k-1})
such that, for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}/k\mathbb{Z}
, the edge
e_{i}
is directed from
e_{i-1}\cap e_{i}
to
e_{i}\cap e_{i+1}
. Observe that, for any sequence of vertices
C=(v_{0},\dots,v_{k-1})
in
Y_{A}
(resp. in
Y_{X}
) with
k\geq 2
, the cycle
(e(v_{0},v_{1}),e(v_{1},v_{2}),\dots,e(v_{k-1},v_{k}))
is the unique directed cycle in
Y_{A}
(resp.
Y_{X}
) with vertices
C
. In both
Y_{A}(n)
and
Y_{X}(n)
, define a sequence
C(n)
as follows. For each
i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
and
j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
, let
y_{i,j}\in e(v_{i},v_{i+1})
be the point such that
d(v_{i},y_{i,j})=j/n
. Let
C(n)\coloneqq(y_{i,j}:i\in\{0,\dots,k-1\},j\in\{0,\dots,n-1\})
, ordered lexicographically. Less formally,
C(n)
is the sequence of points in
Y_{A}(n)
(resp.
Y_{X}(n)
) that one encounters if one starts at
v_{0}
and follows the directed cycle in
Y_{A}
with vertices
C
.

Given
y_{A}\in Y_{A}(n)
, there is a unique
a\in A
such that
y_{A}\in e(b,a)-\{b\}
for some
b\in A
. Define
\varphi_{n}(y_{A})\in\Sym(Y_{X}(n))
as follows. Let
C_{1}\dots C_{k}
be the cyclic decomposition of
\sigma_{a}
; so, for each
i
,
C_{i}=(x_{0},\dots,x_{m})
for some
m\geq 0
and
x_{0},\dots,x_{m-1}\in X
and
\sigma_{a}(x_{j})=x_{j+1}
for each
j\in\mathbb{Z}/(m+1)\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\varphi_{n}(y_{A})
be the symmetry of
Y_{A}(n)
with cyclic decomposition
C_{1}(n)\dots C_{k}(n)
. This defines a homomorphism
\varphi_{n}:\langle Y_{A}(n)\rangle\rightarrow\Sym(Y_{X}(n))
. Note that, if
Y_{X}(n)
is equipped with the metric induced by
Y_{X}
, the action
\varphi_{n}
is
not
by isometries.

Similarly, if
y_{X}\in Y_{X}(n)
, let
x\in X
be the unique vertex of
Y_{X}
such that
y_{X}\in e(z,x)-\{z\}
for some
z\in X
and let
C_{1}\dots C_{k}
be the cyclic decomposition of
\sigma_{x}
. Define
\varphi_{n}(y_{X})\in\Sym(Y_{A}(n))
to be the symmetry of
Y_{A}(n)
with cyclic decomposition
C_{1}(n)\dots C_{k}(n)
. This determines a homomorphism
\varphi_{n}:\langle Y_{X}(n)\rangle\rightarrow\Sym(Y_{A}(n))
.

Definition 6.27
.

For each
y_{A}\in Y_{A}(n),y_{X}\in Y_{X}(n)
, let

r_{n}(y_{A},y_{X})\coloneqq y_{A}\;\varphi_{n}(y_{A})^{-1}(y_{X})\;(\varphi_{n}(y_{X})^{-1}(y_{A}))^{-1}\;y_{X}^{-1}\in\langle Y_{A}(n)\cup Y_{X}(n)\rangle

and let
R(n)\coloneqq\{r_{n}(y_{A},y_{X}):y_{A}\in Y_{A}(n),\;y_{X}\in Y_{X}(n)\}
. Let
G_{n}
be the group with presentation
\langle Y_{A}(n)\cup Y_{X}(n)\;|\;R(n)\rangle
and let
\Gamma_{n}
be the Cayley complex of
G_{n}
with respect to this presentation. The image of a relation
r(y_{A},y_{X})
in
\Gamma_{n}
is illustrated in Figure
2
.

y_{X}
\varphi_{n}(y_{X})^{-1}(y_{A})
\varphi_{n}(y_{A})^{-1}(y_{X})

.

Claim 1
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\langle Y_{A}(n)\cup Y_{X}(n)\;|\;R(n)\rangle
is a BMW presentation.

Proof.

Let
z_{A}\in Y_{A}(n)\cup Y_{A}(n)^{-1},z_{X}\in Y_{X}(n)\cup Y_{X}(n)^{-1}
and let
y_{A}\in Y_{A}(n)
and
y_{X}\in Y_{X}(n)
be such that either
z_{A}=y_{A}
or
z_{A}=y_{A}^{-1}
, and
z_{X}=y_{X}
or
z_{X}=y_{X}^{-1}
. Then
r_{n}(y_{A},y_{X})
is the unique element of
R(n)
of the form
z_{A}z_{X}z_{A}^{\prime}z_{X}^{\prime}
or
z_{A}^{\prime}z_{X}^{\prime}z_{A}z_{X}
or
z_{A}^{-1}z_{X}^{\prime}z_{A}^{\prime}z_{X}^{-1}
or
z_{A}^{\prime}z_{X}^{-1}z_{A}z_{X}^{\prime}
for some
z_{A}^{\prime}\in Y_{A}\cup Y_{A}^{-1}
and
z_{X}^{\prime}\in Y_{X}\cup Y_{X}^{-1}
.
∎

Let
d_{n}
be the path metric on the 1-skeleton
\Gamma_{n}^{(1)}
, where each edge is assigned a length of
1/n
.

Equip
\mathbb{N}
with the partial order
n\preceq m
if and only if
n
divides
m
, and note that
(\mathbb{N},\preceq)
is a net. If
m=kn
, where
k,m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\psi_{n,m}:G_{n}\rightarrow G_{m}
as follows. If
y\in Y_{A}(n)\cup Y_{X}(n)
, set
\psi_{n,m}(y)\coloneqq y^{k}
. If
y_{A}\in Y_{A}(n),y_{X}\in Y_{X}(n)
, then

\displaystyle\psi_{n,m}(r_{n}(y_{A},y_{X}))

\displaystyle=y_{A}^{k}\;\varphi_{n}(y_{A})^{-1}(y_{X})^{k}\;\varphi_{n}(y_{X})^{-1}(y_{A})^{-k}\;y_{X}^{-k}

\displaystyle=y_{A}^{k}\;\varphi_{m}(y_{A})^{-k}(y_{X})^{k}\;\varphi_{m}(y_{X})^{-k}(y_{A})^{-k}\;y_{X}^{-k}.

Let
a\in A
and
x\in X
be such that
y_{A}\in e(a^{\prime},a)-\{a^{\prime}\}
and
y_{X}\in e(x^{\prime},x)-\{x^{\prime}\}
for some
a^{\prime}\in A,x^{\prime}\in X
.
Note that, since
y_{A}\in Y_{A}(n)
, for any
i\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
and for all
z_{X}\in Y_{X}(m)
we have
\varphi_{m}(z_{X})^{i}(y_{A})\in e(a^{\prime},a)-\{a^{\prime}\}
for some
a^{\prime}\in A
. Therefore
\varphi_{j}(\varphi_{m}(z_{X})^{i}(y_{A}))=\varphi_{j}(y_{A})
for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Similarly,
\varphi_{j}(\varphi_{m}(z_{A})^{i}(y_{X}))=\varphi_{j}(y_{x})
for any
z_{A}\in Y_{A}(m),i\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
and
j\in\mathbb{N}
. It follows that
\psi_{n,m}(r(y_{A},y_{X}))=\id
(see Figure
3
). Therefore
\psi_{n,m}
extends a homomorphism
\psi_{n,m}:G_{n}\rightarrow G_{m}
.

By construction,
\psi_{n,m}
is an isometric embedding when restricted to balls of radius 1 in
G_{n}
. It extends naturally to a local isometry
\overline{\psi}_{n,m}:\Gamma_{n}\rightarrow\Gamma_{m}
, where the image
\overline{\psi}_{n,m}(\Gamma_{n})
is the convex hull of
\psi_{n,m}(G_{n})
. Since both
\Gamma_{n}
and
\overline{\psi}_{n,m}(\Gamma_{n})
are simply connected, this implies that
\psi_{n,m}
is an isometric embedding.

\psi_{n,m}
-image of

, where

and

. For each

, we denote
y_{A,i}\coloneqq\varphi_{m}(y_{X})^{-i}(y_{A})
and

.

Definition 6.28
.

Let
G
be the direct union of the system
\langle G_{n},\psi_{n,m}\rangle
.

Since each
\psi_{n,m}
is an isometric embedding, there is a metric
d:G^{2}\rightarrow G
such that the restriction of
d
to
G_{n}^{2}
is
d_{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
X
be the completion of
(G,d)
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\psi_{n}:\langle Y_{A}(n)\cup Y_{X}(n)\rangle\rightarrow G
be the canonical embedding. It is a homomorphism and an isometric embedding.

Claim 2
.

X
is the
\ell^{1}
product of two
\mathbb{R}
trees
T_{A}\times T_{X}
.

Proof.

Let
W_{A}\coloneqq\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}Y_{A}(n)
and
W_{X}\coloneqq\cup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}Y_{X}(n)
. Let
S_{A}\coloneqq\langle W_{A}\rangle\leq G
and
S_{X}\coloneqq\langle W_{X}\rangle\leq G
. We will show that
S_{A}
and
S_{X}
are
\mathbb{Q}
-trees.

Let
f,g,h\in S_{A}
. Then there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
f,g,h\in\langle Y_{A}(n)\rangle
. Note that
d(f,g)=d_{n}(f,g)\in\mathbb{Z}/n\leq\mathbb{Q}
so
S_{A}
is indeed a
\mathbb{Q}
-metric space.
Since
(\langle Y_{A}(n)\rangle,d_{n})
is a
\mathbb{Z}/n
-tree, there exists a median
p\in\langle Y_{A}(n)\rangle
for
d_{n}
and therefore for
d
. For all
m\succeq n
, the restriction of
\psi_{n,m}
to
\langle Y_{A}(n)\rangle
is an isometric embedding into
(\langle Y_{A}(m),d_{m}\rangle
. It follows that
p
is the unique median of
\{f,g,h\}
in
S_{A}
, so
S_{A}
is median, and that
S_{A}
has rank 1. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let
y_{A}\in Y_{A}(n)
. For each
p/q\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,1/n]
such that
p
and
q
are coprime,
\gamma(t)\coloneqq\psi_{q}(y_{A}^{p})
. Let
\gamma(0)\coloneqq\id
. Then
\gamma
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-geodesic in
G
from
\id
to
\psi_{n}(y_{A})
. It follows that
G
is a geodesic
\mathbb{Q}
-metric space. By Lemma
2.11
, this implies that
S_{A}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-tree. A symmetric argument shows that
S_{X}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-tree.

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, Proposition
2.14
implies that
(\langle Y_{A}(n)\cup Y_{X}(n)\rangle,d_{n})
is, as a
\mathbb{Z}/n
-metric space, the
\ell^{1}
product of
\langle Y_{A}(n)\rangle
and
\langle Y_{X}(n)\rangle
. It follows that
G
is the
\ell^{1}
-product of
S_{A}
and
S_{X}
as a
\mathbb{Q}
-metric space. Let
T_{A}
be the completion of
S_{A}
and let
T_{X}
be the completion of
S_{X}
. Then
X
is the
\ell^{1}
product
T_{A}\times T_{X}
.
∎

Since each element of
G
has positive translation length in
G
, the action of
G
on its completion
X
is free.
This proves Item ii of the theorem. The injection
\psi_{1}
verifies Item i, and naturally extends to an
H
-equivariant isometric embedding
\psi:\Cay(H,A\cup X)\hookrightarrow T_{A}\times T_{X}
, verifying Item iii.
The last part of the theorem is proved in the following claim:

Claim 3
.

If
G
contains a subgroup
K\leq G
which splits non-trivially as a direct product such that the induced action
K\curvearrowright T_{A}\times T_{X}
has dense orbits, then
H
is reducible.

Proof.

Note that
G\leq\Isom(T_{A})\times\Isom(T_{X})
. Let
p_{A}:G\rightarrow\Isom(T_{A}),p_{X}:G\rightarrow\Isom(T_{X})
be the canonical projections. Also recall that
(G,d)
is isomorphic to the
\ell^{1}
product
S_{A}\times S_{X}
. Let
\rho_{A}:G\rightarrow S_{A},\rho_{X}:G\rightarrow S_{X}
be the canonical projections.

By Lemma
6.21
,
K\cong K_{A}\times K_{X}
, where
K_{A}\leq\ker(p_{X})\leq S_{A},K_{X}\leq\ker(p_{A})\leq S_{X}
, and
K_{A}
is dense in
T_{A}
, and
K_{X}
is dense in
T_{X}
.

Let
\Gamma_{A},\Gamma_{X}
be the Cayley graphs of the free groups
\langle A\rangle,\langle X\rangle
with respect to the generating sets
A,X
.
Let
q_{A}:H\rightarrow\Isom(\Gamma_{A})
and
q_{X}:H\rightarrow\Isom(\Gamma_{X})
be the canonical projections. We will show that
q_{A}(H)
and
q_{X}(H)
are discrete with respect to the compact open topology on
\Isom(\Gamma_{A})
and
\Isom(\Gamma_{X})
. By
[
BM00
, Proposition 1.2]
, this implies that
H
is reducible.

Let
h\in H
be such that
q_{X}(h)
fixes the ball of radius
1
in
\Gamma_{X}
around
\id_{X}
pointwise. In particular,
q_{X}(h)
fixes
\id_{X}
, so
h\in\langle A\rangle
. Moreover
h\cdot x=x
for all
x\in X
. Then, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the element
\varphi_{n}(\psi_{1,n}(h))\in\Sym(Y_{X}(n))
is the identity map. Let
f\coloneqq\psi_{1}(h)
. Then
p_{X}(f)(g)=g
for all
g\in S_{X}
with
d(g,\id_{X})\leq 1
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and suppose that
p_{X}(f)(g)=g
for all
g\in S_{X}
such that
d(g,\id_{X})<n
. Let
g\in S_{X}
be such that
d(g,\id_{X})<n+1
. There exists
k_{X}\in K_{X}
such that
d(k_{X},\id_{X})<n
and
d(k_{X},g_{X})<1
. Then
p_{X}(f)(k_{X})=k_{X}
, which, since
p_{A}(k_{X})(f)=f
, implies that
f
and
k_{X}
commute. Let
g^{\prime}\coloneqq k_{X}^{-1}g
, so
d(\id_{x},g)<1
. Then

p_{X}(f)(g)=\rho_{X}(fk_{X}g^{\prime})=\rho_{X}(k_{X}fg^{\prime})=k_{X}p_{X}(f)(g^{\prime})=k_{X}g^{\prime}=g.

It follows by induction on
n
that
p_{X}(f)
is the trivial map on
S_{X}
. Since
\psi
is
H
-equivariant, this implies in particular that
q_{X}(h)
is the identity map on
\Gamma_{X}
. Therefore the identity is isolated in
q_{X}(H)
, so
q_{X}(H)
is discrete. A symmetric argument shows that
q_{A}(H)
is discrete.
∎
